M12. Estimate first (round each number to its biggest place). Then work out the
     exact answer.
     a) 68 × 32     b) 49 × 21     c) 57 × 43     d) 82 × 19     e) 38 × 45
     f) 91 × 28     g) 64 × 66     h) 27 × 73     i) 7 × 386     j) 8 × 2,478
     k) 6 × 512     l) 9 × 719     m) 124 × 23    n) 356 × 24    o) 498 × 51
     p) 703 × 38
     Answers:
       a) 70 × 30 = 2,100; exact 2,176
       b) 50 × 20 = 1,000; exact 1,029
       c) 60 × 40 = 2,400; exact 2,451
       d) 80 × 20 = 1,600; exact 1,558
       e) 40 × 50 = 2,000; exact 1,710
       f) 90 × 30 = 2,700; exact 2,548
       g) 60 × 70 = 4,200; exact 4,224
       h) 30 × 70 = 2,100; exact 1,971
       i) 7 × 400 = 2,800; exact 2,702
       j) 8 × 2,000 = 16,000; exact 19,824
       k) 6 × 500 = 3,000; exact 3,072
       l) 9 × 700 = 6,300; exact 6,471
       m) 100 × 20 = 2,000; exact 2,852
       n) 400 × 20 = 8,000; exact 8,544
       o) 500 × 50 = 25,000; exact 25,398
       p) 700 × 40 = 28,000; exact 26,714

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: stay with partial products and the area model (draw the rectangle
  on squared paper) until the child can explain every row; practise
  2-digit × 1-digit with carrying for a week before 2-digit × 2-digit; write the
  placeholder zero in a different colour; always estimate first. Harder:
  3-digit × 2-digit and 4-digit × 2-digit problems; find the missing digits in a
  long multiplication; use the grid of the area model to multiply 3-digit by
  3-digit numbers (125 × 125 = 15,625); check answers by swapping the order of
  the numbers.


================================================================================
TOPIC 4.8 — DIVISION FACTS; DIVISION WITH REMAINDERS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - Know EVERY division fact that comes from the times tables up to 12 × 12,
    in order and mixed up: 56 ÷ 8 = 7, 144 ÷ 12 = 12, 99 ÷ 11 = 9. Think "8
    times what is 56?"
  - Use the words: in 56 ÷ 8 = 7, 56 is the DIVIDEND (the number we share), 8
    is the DIVISOR (what we divide by) and 7 is the QUOTIENT (the answer).
  - When a number does not divide exactly, the amount left over is the
    REMAINDER. We write 47 ÷ 5 = 9 R 2 ("9 remainder 2"), because 9 × 5 = 45
    and 2 more makes 47.
  - The remainder is ALWAYS smaller than the divisor. When you divide by 6 the
    only possible remainders are 0, 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5. A remainder of 0 means
    the division is exact.
  - Check every division: quotient × divisor + remainder = dividend.
  - In a story, think about what the remainder means: round UP (how many vans
    are needed), round DOWN (how many full boxes), the remainder IS the answer
    (how many are left), or share it as a fraction (half a roti each).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Amma has 56 laddoos for the Diwali party. She shares them equally among 8
    children. How many laddoos does each child get?
    Answer: 7 laddoos each. 56 ÷ 8 = 7.
    Teacher explains: This is sharing among 8. We do not need to deal out 56
    laddoos one by one: we use the 8 times table. Ask, "8 times WHAT makes
    56?" Say the 8 table: 8, 16, 24, 32, 40, 48, 56 — that is 7 eights. So
    7 × 8 = 56 and 56 ÷ 8 = 7. Every multiplication fact gives us two division
    facts for free: 7 × 8 = 56 gives 56 ÷ 8 = 7 and 56 ÷ 7 = 8. Check: 8
    children with 7 each is 8 × 7 = 56. Now you try: 63 laddoos among 9
    children. (7 each)

D2. A shop packs eggs in trays of 12. How many trays are needed for 72 eggs?
    Answer: 6 trays. 72 ÷ 12 = 6.
    Teacher explains: This is grouping: we know the size of each group (12)
    and want HOW MANY groups. Count in 12s and keep a finger up for each tray:
    12, 24, 36, 48, 60, 72 — 6 fingers. So 6 × 12 = 72 and 72 ÷ 12 = 6. A
    quicker way for the 12 table: 12 = 10 + 2, so 6 × 12 = 60 + 12 = 72. Learn
    the 11 and 12 tables as well as the others, because they give facts like
    132 ÷ 12 = 11 and 144 ÷ 12 = 12. Now you try: 84 eggs in trays of 12. (7
    trays)

D3. At a school cricket tournament there are 132 players. Each team has 11
    players. How many teams are there?
    Answer: 12 teams. 132 ÷ 11 = 12.
    Teacher explains: Think: "11 times what is 132?" The 11 table to 9 is easy
    (11, 22, ... 99). After that: 10 × 11 = 110, 11 × 11 = 121, 12 × 11 = 132.
    So 132 ÷ 11 = 12. Check: 12 × 11 = 120 + 12 = 132. Big division facts are
    just the times tables read backwards. Now you try: 121 players in teams of
    11. (11 teams)

D4. The teacher has 47 pencils. She ties them in packs of 5. How many packs
    can she make? How many pencils are left over?
    Answer: 9 packs, and 2 pencils are left over. 47 ÷ 5 = 9 R 2.
    Teacher explains: Count in 5s but do not go past 47: 5, 10, 15, 20, 25,
    30, 35, 40, 45 — that is 9 fives, and the next five (50) is too many.
    9 × 5 = 45, and 47 − 45 = 2 are left. We write 47 ÷ 5 = 9 R 2 and say "9
    remainder 2". Always check: quotient × divisor + remainder = dividend:
    9 × 5 + 2 = 45 + 2 = 47. A good question to ask is "Which is the biggest
    number in the 5 table that is not more than 47?" Now you try: 38 pencils
    in packs of 5. (7 packs, 3 left: 7 R 3)

D5. Tina works out 29 ÷ 4 and writes 6 R 5. What is wrong? What is the right
    answer?
    Answer: The remainder 5 is bigger than the divisor 4, so she could have
    made one more group of 4. 29 ÷ 4 = 7 R 1.
    Teacher explains: Tina's sum is not false (6 × 4 + 5 = 29), but it is not
    finished. If 5 are left and each group needs 4, we can make ONE more group
    and 1 is left: 7 groups and 1 left over. Rule: the remainder must always
    be SMALLER than the divisor. If it is not, add 1 to the quotient and take
    the divisor away from the remainder. Check: 7 × 4 + 1 = 28 + 1 = 29. Now
    you try: fix 40 ÷ 6 = 5 R 10. (6 R 4)

D6. Divide the numbers 21, 22, 23, 24, 25, 26, 27 and 28 by 7. What remainders
    do you get? Can you ever get a remainder of 7 or more?
    Answer: 21 ÷ 7 = 3, 22 ÷ 7 = 3 R 1, 23 ÷ 7 = 3 R 2, 24 ÷ 7 = 3 R 3,
    25 ÷ 7 = 3 R 4, 26 ÷ 7 = 3 R 5, 27 ÷ 7 = 3 R 6, 28 ÷ 7 = 4. The only
    remainders when dividing by 7 are 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 and 6. Never 7 or more.
    Teacher explains: Put the numbers in a row and watch the remainder grow by
    1 each time: 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6 — and then, at 28, it goes back to 0
    because 28 is the next number in the 7 table. The remainder is like a
    counter that starts again every 7 steps. So dividing by 7 has 7 possible
    remainders (0 to 6), dividing by 4 has 4 (0 to 3), and dividing by 12 has
    12 (0 to 11). Now you try: which remainders can you get when you divide by
    4? (0, 1, 2, 3)

D7. A school van can carry 8 children. 50 children are going on a trip. How
    many vans are needed?
    Answer: 7 vans. 50 ÷ 8 = 6 R 2, so 6 vans are full and 1 more van is
    needed for the last 2 children.
    Teacher explains: Work it out first: 6 × 8 = 48, and 50 − 48 = 2, so
    50 ÷ 8 = 6 R 2. But the answer to the STORY is not 6! Two children cannot
    be left at school, so they need another van. Here we ROUND UP: 6 + 1 = 7
    vans. Ask the child, "What does the remainder 2 mean here?" (two children
    with no seat). Now you try: 45 children, 6 in each boat. How many boats?
    (45 ÷ 6 = 7 R 3, so 8 boats)

D8. Riya has ₹50. A notebook costs ₹8. How many notebooks can she buy?
    Answer: 6 notebooks (with ₹2 left). 50 ÷ 8 = 6 R 2.
    Teacher explains: 6 × 8 = 48, and 50 − 48 = 2, so 50 ÷ 8 = 6 R 2. This
    time we ROUND DOWN: ₹2 is not enough for a 7th notebook, so she buys 6 and
    keeps ₹2. Compare with D7: the SAME sum, 50 ÷ 8 = 6 R 2, gives 7 vans but
    only 6 notebooks! Always read the question again and decide. Now you try:
    ₹75, and a pen costs ₹9. How many pens? (75 ÷ 9 = 8 R 3, so 8 pens)

D9. A sweet shop has 100 laddoos. They fill boxes of 12. How many laddoos are
    left over after all the full boxes are packed?
    Answer: 4 laddoos are left over. 100 ÷ 12 = 8 R 4.
    Teacher explains: 8 × 12 = 96 and 9 × 12 = 108 is too many, so 8 full
    boxes are made. 100 − 96 = 4. The question asks how many are LEFT, so here
    the REMAINDER itself is the answer, not the quotient. Check:
    8 × 12 + 4 = 96 + 4 = 100. Now you try: 60 marbles are put in bags of 7.
    How many marbles are left over? (60 ÷ 7 = 8 R 4, so 4 left)

D10. Nani shares 9 chapatis equally between 2 children. She cuts any left-over
     chapati into equal pieces. How much does each child get?
     Answer: 4 and a half chapatis each. 9 ÷ 2 = 4 R 1, and the 1 left over is
     cut into 2 halves.
     Teacher explains: Each child gets 4 whole chapatis (4 × 2 = 8), and 1 is
     left over. A chapati CAN be cut, so we do not throw it away: cut it into
     2 equal halves and give one half to each child. So each gets 4 and 1/2.
     When the things can be cut (chapatis, cakes, ribbon), we can share the
     remainder as a FRACTION: remainder ÷ divisor, here 1 out of 2 = 1/2.
     Children cannot be cut, so in D7 we had to round up instead! Now you try:
     13 cakes shared equally among 4 friends. (3 and 1/4 cakes each)

D11. Arjun says 54 ÷ 6 = 8. Is he right? How can he check?
     Answer: No. 8 × 6 = 48, not 54. The right answer is 54 ÷ 6 = 9, because
     9 × 6 = 54.
     Teacher explains: Every division can be checked by multiplying back:
     quotient × divisor should give the dividend. 8 × 6 = 48, which is 6 less
     than 54, so Arjun is one 6 short. 9 × 6 = 54, so the answer is 9.
     Mistakes like this happen when the 6, 7, 8 and 9 tables are not yet
     quick. Practise them every day and always check. Now you try: is
     63 ÷ 7 = 8? (No: 8 × 7 = 56. 63 ÷ 7 = 9)

D12. Work out 85 ÷ 9. Name the dividend, divisor, quotient and remainder, and
     check your answer.
     Answer: 85 ÷ 9 = 9 R 4. Dividend 85, divisor 9, quotient 9, remainder 4.
     Check: 9 × 9 + 4 = 81 + 4 = 85.
     Teacher explains: Find the biggest number in the 9 table that is not more
     than 85: ..., 72, 81, 90. 90 is too big, so use 81 = 9 × 9. The quotient
     is 9. The remainder is 85 − 81 = 4, and 4 is smaller than 9, so it is
     allowed. Write it as a sentence with all four names: "85 divided by 9 is
     9, remainder 4." Then check by multiplying and adding. Now you try:
     70 ÷ 8. (8 R 6, because 8 × 8 + 6 = 70)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Divide. All 24 facts of the 2 and 3 tables (÷ 2 and ÷ 3), mixed. Think "3
    times what?"

M4. Divide. All 24 facts of the 8 and 9 tables (÷ 8 and ÷ 9), mixed.
    a) 80 ÷ 8    b) 96 ÷ 8    c) 88 ÷ 8    d) 72 ÷ 8    e) 32 ÷ 8    f) 72 ÷ 9

M7. Find the missing number. Use the times tables.

     a) __ ÷ 7 = 8 R 3      b) __ ÷ 9 = 6 R 5      c) __ ÷ 4 = 9 R 3

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: go back to the 2, 5 and 10 facts, then learn one new table a
  week using multiplication cards turned round ("8 times what is 56?"); act
  out remainders with counters and cups, and always say what the left-over
  means. Harder: remainders with dividends up to 200 (200 ÷ 12), stories where
  the child must choose round up, round down or share the remainder, and
  puzzles like "a number divided by 5 leaves remainder 3 and divided by 4
  leaves remainder 2: find it" (18, 38, 58 ...).


================================================================================
TOPIC 4.9 — LONG DIVISION (1-DIGIT DIVISOR, THEN 2-DIGIT)
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - Long division shares out one place at a time, starting from the BIGGEST
    place on the LEFT: thousands, then hundreds, then tens, then ones.
  - The four steps, again and again: DIVIDE, MULTIPLY, SUBTRACT, BRING DOWN
    (remember "Does Mummy Serve Biryani?"). Write each quotient digit above
    the digit you have just used.
  - If the first digit is too small (2 hundreds ÷ 6), use the first TWO digits
    (25 tens ÷ 6). If a part is too small later on, write 0 in the quotient
    and bring down the next digit: 612 ÷ 6 = 102, 840 ÷ 4 = 210. Never skip
    the 0.
  - Short division (the "bus stop"): for a 1-digit divisor you can write the
    carried remainders as small digits instead of writing every subtraction.
  - Divide 2-, 3- and 4-digit numbers by a 1-digit number, with and without
    remainders; then 3-digit numbers by a 2-digit number (544 ÷ 17 = 32),
    using a list of multiples of the divisor or by rounding the divisor to
    estimate each digit.
  - Estimate first (612 ÷ 6 is about 600 ÷ 6 = 100) so you know how many
    digits the answer has, and check at the end: quotient × divisor +
    remainder = dividend.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. 72 children go on a picnic in 3 buses, with the same number in each bus.
    How many children are in each bus?
            2 4
           ----
        3 ) 7 2
          − 6
            -
            1 2
          − 1 2
            ---
              0
    Answer: 24 children. 72 ÷ 3 = 24.
    Teacher explains: Start with the TENS: 7 tens ÷ 3 = 2 tens each
    (2 × 3 = 6), write 2 above the 7. Subtract: 7 − 6 = 1 ten left over. Bring
    down the 2 ones next to it: 1 ten and 2 ones is 12 ones. 12 ÷ 3 = 4, write
    4 above the 2. 4 × 3 = 12, 12 − 12 = 0. Answer 24. With base-ten blocks:
    share 7 rods among 3 plates (2 each), change the extra rod into 10 cubes,
    and share the 12 cubes (4 each). Check: 24 × 3 = 72. Now you try: 96 ÷ 4.
    (24)

D2. A bangle seller has 87 bangles. She packs them in boxes of 4. How many
    full boxes does she make, and how many bangles are left over?
            2 1
           ----
        4 ) 8 7
          − 8
            -
            0 7
            − 4
              -
              3
    Answer: 21 boxes, and 3 bangles left over. 87 ÷ 4 = 21 R 3.
    Teacher explains: Tens: 8 ÷ 4 = 2, write 2. 2 × 4 = 8, 8 − 8 = 0. Bring
    down the 7: 07, that is 7. 7 ÷ 4 = 1 R 3: write 1, as 1 × 4 = 4 and
    7 − 4 = 3. There are no more digits to bring down, so 3 is the REMAINDER.
    It is smaller than 4, good. Write 87 ÷ 4 = 21 R 3. Check:
    21 × 4 + 3 = 84 + 3 = 87. Now you try: 59 ÷ 3. (19 R 2)

D3. A farmer has 745 kg of rice. He fills 5 equal sacks. How much rice goes in
    each sack?
            1 4 9
           ------
        5 ) 7 4 5
          − 5
            -
            2 4
          − 2 0
            ---
              4 5
            − 4 5
              ---
                0
    Answer: 149 kg. 745 ÷ 5 = 149.
    Teacher explains: Estimate: 750 ÷ 5 is 150, so the answer is about 150.
    Hundreds: 7 ÷ 5 = 1 R 2, write 1; 1 × 5 = 5, 7 − 5 = 2. Bring down 4: 24.
    24 ÷ 5 = 4 R 4, write 4; 4 × 5 = 20, 24 − 20 = 4. Bring down 5: 45.
    45 ÷ 5 = 9, write 9; 9 × 5 = 45, 45 − 45 = 0. Answer 149, close to our
    estimate of 150. Check: 149 × 5 = 745. Say the four steps aloud each time:
    divide, multiply, subtract, bring down. Now you try: 636 ÷ 4. (159)

D4. 258 children are put into 6 equal groups for a sports day. How many
    children are in each group?
              4 3
           ------
        6 ) 2 5 8
          − 2 4
            ---
              1 8
            − 1 8
              ---
                0
    Answer: 43 children. 258 ÷ 6 = 43.
    Teacher explains: Hundreds: 2 ÷ 6? 2 hundreds cannot be shared among 6
    without changing them, so we use the first TWO digits: 25 tens.
    25 ÷ 6 = 4 R 1 (4 × 6 = 24), write 4 above the 5 (not above the 2!).
    25 − 24 = 1. Bring down 8: 18. 18 ÷ 6 = 3, write 3. 3 × 6 = 18,
    18 − 18 = 0. Answer 43, a 2-digit number, because the hundreds were too
    small. Estimate: 240 ÷ 6 = 40, close. Check: 43 × 6 = 258. Now you try:
    184 ÷ 8. (23)

D5. 612 mangoes are packed equally into 6 crates. How many mangoes go in each
    crate?
            1 0 2
           ------
        6 ) 6 1 2
          − 6
            -
            0 1
            − 0
              -
              1 2
            − 1 2
              ---
                0
    Answer: 102 mangoes. 612 ÷ 6 = 102.
    Teacher explains: Hundreds: 6 ÷ 6 = 1, write 1; 6 − 6 = 0. Bring down 1:
    we have just 1 ten. 1 ÷ 6 = 0 R 1, so write 0 in the tens place of the
    answer — this 0 is IMPORTANT. 0 × 6 = 0, 1 − 0 = 1. Bring down 2: 12.
    12 ÷ 6 = 2, write 2. Answer 102. A common mistake is to write 12,
    forgetting the 0. The estimate catches it: 600 ÷ 6 = 100, so the answer
    must be about 100, not 12. Check: 102 × 6 = 612. Now you try: 816 ÷ 4.
    (204)

D6. A school spends ₹840 on 4 equal parcels of books. How much does each
    parcel cost?
            2 1 0
           ------
        4 ) 8 4 0
          − 8
            -
            0 4
            − 4
              -
              0 0
              − 0
                -
                0
    Answer: ₹210. 840 ÷ 4 = 210.
    Teacher explains: Hundreds: 8 ÷ 4 = 2, write 2. Tens: bring down 4,
    4 ÷ 4 = 1, write 1. Ones: bring down 0. 0 ÷ 4 = 0, so write 0 in the ones
    place of the answer. Answer 210, not 21! Every place of the dividend after
    we start must have a digit in the quotient. Think of it as 84 tens ÷
    4 = 21 tens = 210. Check: 210 × 4 = 840. Now you try: 930 ÷ 3. (310)

D7. 953 mangoes are packed in crates of 7. How many full crates are there, and
    how many mangoes are left over?
            1 3 6
           ------
        7 ) 9 5 3
          − 7
            -
            2 5
          − 2 1
            ---
              4 3
            − 4 2
              ---
                1
    Answer: 136 full crates, and 1 mango left over. 953 ÷ 7 = 136 R 1.
    Teacher explains: 9 ÷ 7 = 1 R 2. Bring down 5: 25 ÷ 7 = 3 R 4
    (3 × 7 = 21). Bring down 3: 43 ÷ 7 = 6 R 1 (6 × 7 = 42). No digits are
    left, so the remainder is 1. Check: 136 × 7 = 952, and 952 + 1 = 953.
    Remind the child: at each step the remainder must be smaller than 7. If
    you ever get 7 or more, the quotient digit is too small. Now you try:
    500 ÷ 3. (166 R 2)

D8. ₹4,728 prize money is shared equally among 8 winners. How much does each
    winner get?
              5 9 1
           --------
        8 ) 4 7 2 8
          − 4 0
            ---
              7 2
            − 7 2
              ---
                0 8
                − 8
                  -
                  0
    Answer: ₹591. 4,728 ÷ 8 = 591.
    Teacher explains: Thousands: 4 ÷ 8 is too small, so use 47 hundreds.
    47 ÷ 8 = 5 R 7 (5 × 8 = 40). Bring down 2: 72 ÷ 8 = 9 exactly. Bring down
    8: 8 ÷ 8 = 1. Answer 591. Because we started with 47 hundreds, the answer
    is in hundreds: 3 digits, not 4. Estimate: 4,800 ÷ 8 = 600, close. Check:
    591 × 8 = 4,728. Now you try: 3,915 ÷ 5. (783)

D9. Show 1,752 ÷ 6 as a short division (the "bus stop" method).
                2  9  2
           ------------
        6 )  1 ¹7 ⁵5 ¹2
    Answer: 292. 1,752 ÷ 6 = 292.
    Teacher explains: Short division is long division with the multiplying and
    subtracting done in your head. 1 ÷ 6 = 0 R 1 (write nothing), carry the 1
    to make 17. 17 ÷ 6 = 2 R 5: write 2 on top and a small 5 in front of the
    next digit, making 55. 55 ÷ 6 = 9 R 1: write 9, carry a small 1, making
    12. 12 ÷ 6 = 2: write 2. Answer 292. The small digits are the remainders
    carried along. Use short division only when the tables are quick;
    otherwise use the long layout. Check: 292 × 6 = 1,752. Now you try:
    2,345 ÷ 5 as a bus stop. (469)

D10. 544 chairs are set out in rows of 17 for a school function. How many rows
     are there?
                3 2
             ------
         17 ) 5 4 4
            − 5 1
              ---
                3 4
              − 3 4
                ---
                  0
     Answer: 32 rows. 544 ÷ 17 = 32.
     Teacher explains: Dividing by a 2-digit number: first write a list of
     multiples of 17 so you do not have to guess: 17, 34, 51, 68, 85, 102,
     119, 136, 153. Now 5 ÷ 17 is too small, so use 54. The biggest multiple
     not more than 54 is 51 = 3 × 17: write 3 above the 4. 54 − 51 = 3. Bring
     down 4: 34. From the list, 34 = 2 × 17: write 2. 34 − 34 = 0. Answer 32.
     Check: 32 × 17 = 510 + 34 = 544. Now you try: 391 ÷ 17. (23)

D11. 850 notebooks are tied in bundles of 24. How many full bundles are made,
     and how many notebooks are left over?
                3 5
             ------
         24 ) 8 5 0
            − 7 2
              ---
              1 3 0
            − 1 2 0
              -----
                1 0
     Answer: 35 bundles, and 10 notebooks left over. 850 ÷ 24 = 35 R 10.
     Teacher explains: Estimate each digit by ROUNDING the divisor: 24 is
     about 20 (or 25). 85 ÷ 24: think 85 ÷ 20, about 4. Try 4: 4 × 24 = 96,
     too big! So try 3: 3 × 24 = 72, and 85 − 72 = 13 (smaller than 24, good).
     Bring down 0: 130. 130 ÷ 24: 130 ÷ 25 is about 5. Try 5: 5 × 24 = 120,
     and 130 − 120 = 10. Answer 35 R 10. If a trial is too big, go one
     smaller; if the remainder is 24 or more, go one bigger. Check:
     35 × 24 = 840, and 840 + 10 = 850. Now you try: 700 ÷ 32. (21 R 28)

D12. Kiran works out 624 ÷ 6 and gets 14. How do you know at once that he is
     wrong? What is the right answer?
             1 0 4
            ------
         6 ) 6 2 4
           − 6
             -
             0 2
             − 0
               -
               2 4
             − 2 4
               ---
                 0
     Answer: 600 ÷ 6 = 100, so the answer must be more than 100. Kiran forgot
     the 0 in the tens place. 624 ÷ 6 = 104.
     Teacher explains: Estimate first and the mistake jumps out: 14 × 6 = 84,
     nowhere near 624. In the long division, after 6 ÷ 6 = 1 we bring down the
     2: 2 ÷ 6 = 0 R 2, and that 0 MUST be written in the quotient. Then bring
     down 4: 24 ÷ 6 = 4. Answer 104. Check: 104 × 6 = 624. Two habits stop
     this mistake: estimate before you divide, and multiply back afterwards.
     Now you try: 927 ÷ 9. (103)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: use base-ten blocks and plates to share hundreds, then tens,
  then ones, changing left-overs into the next place; write the steps D-M-S-B
  down the side of the page; use squared paper so every digit sits in its own
  column; stay with 1-digit divisors and no remainders until the steps are
  automatic. Harder: 4-digit ÷ 2-digit (6,072 ÷ 23), dividing by numbers like
  25 and 99 by clever methods, and story problems where the child must decide
  what the remainder means.


================================================================================
TOPIC 4.10 — RELATIONSHIP BETWEEN MULTIPLICATION AND DIVISION
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - Multiplication and division are INVERSE operations: one undoes the other.
    6 × 7 = 42, so 42 ÷ 7 = 6. Multiply by 7 and then divide by 7, and you are
    back where you started.
  - Three numbers make a FACT FAMILY of two multiplications and two divisions:
    4 × 9 = 36, 9 × 4 = 36, 36 ÷ 4 = 9, 36 ÷ 9 = 4. A square such as
    8 × 8 = 64 has only two facts.
  - Find a missing number in ANY position by using the inverse: __ × 8 = 56
    (56 ÷ 8 = 7); 72 ÷ __ = 9 (72 ÷ 9 = 8); __ ÷ 6 = 9 (9 × 6 = 54);
    __ ÷ 5 = 7 R 3 (7 × 5 + 3 = 38).
  - Check a division by multiplying, and a multiplication by dividing.
  - Dividing by 1 changes nothing (9 ÷ 1 = 9). A number divided by itself is 1
    (9 ÷ 9 = 1). 0 divided by any number is 0 (0 ÷ 9 = 0).
  - We can NEVER divide by 0: 6 ÷ 0 has no answer, because no number times 0
    makes 6.
  - Order matters in division but not in multiplication: 3 × 12 = 12 × 3, but
    12 ÷ 3 is not the same as 3 ÷ 12.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. In the school hall there are 4 rows of chairs with 9 chairs in each row.
    Write all the multiplication and division facts that the chairs show.
    Answer: 4 × 9 = 36, 9 × 4 = 36, 36 ÷ 4 = 9, 36 ÷ 9 = 4.
    Teacher explains: Draw the array: 4 rows of 9 dots. Counting the rows
    first gives 4 × 9 = 36; counting the columns first gives 9 × 4 = 36. Now
    divide the SAME 36 chairs: sharing them into 4 rows gives 9 in each row
    (36 ÷ 4 = 9), and grouping them in 4s (the columns) gives 9 groups
    (36 ÷ 9 = 4). One picture, four facts. The biggest number, 36, is the
    answer in a multiplication and the starting number in a division. Now you
    try: the family for 6, 8 and 48. (6 × 8 = 48, 8 × 6 = 48, 48 ÷ 6 = 8,
    48 ÷ 8 = 6)

D2. Ravi packs 63 samosas into boxes of 7. He writes __ × 7 = 63. How many
    boxes does he need?
    Answer: 9 boxes. 9 × 7 = 63, found by 63 ÷ 7 = 9.
    Teacher explains: A missing FACTOR is found by dividing, because division
    undoes multiplication. __ × 7 = 63 asks "how many 7s make 63?", which is
    exactly 63 ÷ 7. Say the 7 table to 63: 9 sevens. Put 9 in the box and read
    the sentence again: 9 × 7 = 63, true. Now you try: __ × 6 = 54. (9)

D3. Priya shares 48 stickers equally among some friends. Each friend gets 6
    stickers. How many friends are there?
    Answer: 8 friends. 48 ÷ __ = 6, and 48 ÷ 6 = 8.
    Teacher explains: Write the story as 48 ÷ __ = 6: the missing number is
    the DIVISOR. Turn it into a multiplication: __ × 6 = 48. "What times 6 is
    48?" 8. So 48 ÷ 8 = 6. There is a neat shortcut: when the divisor is
    missing, swap it with the answer: 48 ÷ 6 = 8. Check: 8 friends with 6 each
    is 48 stickers. Now you try: 45 ÷ __ = 5. (9)

D4. Some sweets were shared equally among 7 children. Each child got 8 sweets
    and none were left. How many sweets were there?
    Answer: 56 sweets. __ ÷ 7 = 8, and 8 × 7 = 56.
    Teacher explains: Here the missing number is the DIVIDEND, the number we
    started with. To undo "÷ 7" we MULTIPLY by 7: 8 × 7 = 56. Think of the
    children putting their sweets back together: 7 piles of 8 make 56. Check:
    56 ÷ 7 = 8. A common mistake is to divide again (8 ÷ 7) — ask, "Should the
    starting number be bigger or smaller than 8?" Bigger! Now you try:
    __ ÷ 9 = 12. (108)

D5. Meera put her marbles into bags of 5. She filled 7 bags and had 3 marbles
    left over. How many marbles did she have?
    Answer: 38 marbles. __ ÷ 5 = 7 R 3, and 7 × 5 + 3 = 35 + 3 = 38.
    Teacher explains: Undo the division step by step. The 7 full bags hold
    7 × 5 = 35 marbles, and the 3 left over are added on: 35 + 3 = 38. So
    dividend = quotient × divisor + remainder. Check: 38 ÷ 5 = 7 R 3, because
    5 × 7 = 35 and 38 − 35 = 3. Now you try: bags of 6, 9 bags full and 4 left
    over. (58 marbles)

D6. Meena says 96 ÷ 8 = 12. Raj says 84 ÷ 6 = 13. Check both answers using
    multiplication.
    Answer: Meena is right: 12 × 8 = 96. Raj is wrong: 13 × 6 = 78, not 84.
    84 ÷ 6 = 14, because 14 × 6 = 84.
    Teacher explains: Multiplication is the quick test for any division:
    quotient × divisor must give back the dividend. 12 × 8 = 80 + 16 = 96,
    correct. 13 × 6 = 60 + 18 = 78, which is 6 short of 84, so Raj needs one
    more 6: 14. Teach the child to check EVERY division like this before
    saying it is finished. Now you try: is 91 ÷ 7 = 13? (Yes, 13 × 7 = 91)

D7. Anu knows that 25 × 4 = 100. Which two division facts does she get for
    free?
    Answer: 100 ÷ 4 = 25 and 100 ÷ 25 = 4.
    Teacher explains: Every multiplication fact, even with big numbers, gives
    a whole fact family. 25 × 4 = 100 means 4 groups of 25 make 100, so
    sharing 100 into 4 groups gives 25 (100 ÷ 4 = 25), and making groups of 25
    from 100 gives 4 groups (100 ÷ 25 = 4). With money: 4 notes of ₹25 would
    make ₹100. Use known facts like this to divide in your head. Now you try:
    125 × 8 = 1,000, so 1,000 ÷ 8 = __. (125)

D8. A monkey has 15 bananas. (a) He keeps them all for himself. (b) He shares
    them among 15 monkeys. How many bananas does each monkey get?
    Answer: (a) 15 bananas: 15 ÷ 1 = 15. (b) 1 banana each: 15 ÷ 15 = 1.
    Teacher explains: Dividing by 1 means sharing among just one, so nothing
    changes: n ÷ 1 = n. Check with multiplication: 15 × 1 = 15. Sharing a
    number among the same number of people gives exactly 1 each: n ÷ n = 1.
    Check: 1 × 15 = 15. These rules work for any number (except 0 in the
    second one): 358 ÷ 1 = 358 and 358 ÷ 358 = 1. Now you try: 23 ÷ 1 and
    23 ÷ 23. (23 and 1)

D9. The laddoo tin is empty: 0 laddoos. Amma shares them among 5 children. How
    many does each child get?
    Answer: 0 laddoos each. 0 ÷ 5 = 0.
    Teacher explains: If there is nothing to share, everybody gets nothing,
    however many children there are. Check with multiplication: 0 × 5 = 0,
    true. So 0 divided by any number (except 0) is 0. Now you try: 0 ÷ 12. (0)

D10. Why can we not work out 6 ÷ 0?
     Answer: Because no number times 0 makes 6. So 6 ÷ 0 has no answer. We say
     we cannot divide by 0.
     Teacher explains: Use the inverse. If 6 ÷ 0 were some number, then that
     number × 0 would have to be 6. But ANY number × 0 is 0, never 6. So there
     is no answer. Think of sharing: 6 laddoos shared among 0 children — there
     is nobody to give them to, so the question makes no sense. Do not mix it
     up with 0 ÷ 6 = 0, which is fine (nothing shared among 6). 0 ÷ 0 is not
     allowed either: every number times 0 is 0, so there is no single answer.
     Now you try: which has an answer, 0 ÷ 4 or 4 ÷ 0? (0 ÷ 4 = 0; 4 ÷ 0 has
     no answer)

D11. Is 7 × 3 the same as 3 × 7? Is 21 ÷ 3 the same as 3 ÷ 21?
     Answer: 7 × 3 = 3 × 7 = 21, yes. But 21 ÷ 3 = 7, while 3 ÷ 21 is much
     less than 1 (3 things shared among 21 people). Not the same.
     Teacher explains: In multiplication the order does not matter: 7 rows of
     3 or 3 rows of 7 are the same 21 dots. In division the order DOES matter:
     sharing 21 laddoos among 3 children is not the same as sharing 3 laddoos
     among 21 children! In a fact family the biggest number comes first in
     each division: 21 ÷ 3 = 7 and 21 ÷ 7 = 3. Now you try: is 8 × 5 = 5 × 8?
     Is 40 ÷ 8 = 8 ÷ 40? (Yes; no)

D12. Think of a number. Multiply it by 4, then divide by 3. The answer is 8.
     What was the number?
     Answer: 6. Undo the steps backwards: 8 × 3 = 24, then 24 ÷ 4 = 6.
     Teacher explains: Work BACKWARDS and do the opposite of each step. The
     last step was "÷ 3", so undo it with × 3: 8 × 3 = 24. The step before was
     "× 4", so undo it with ÷ 4: 24 ÷ 4 = 6. Check forwards: 6 × 4 = 24,
     24 ÷ 3 = 8. Draw it as a flow: 6 → × 4 → 24 → ÷ 3 → 8, and backwards 8 →
     × 3 → 24 → ÷ 4 → 6. Now you try: I divide my number by 5, then multiply
     by 3, and get 27. (45)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Write the fact family (two × and two ÷) for each set of three numbers. l)
    is a square.
    a) 2, 7, 14     b) 3, 8, 24     c) 4, 6, 24     d) 5, 9, 45
    e) 6, 7, 42     f) 7, 8, 56     g) 8, 9, 72     h) 9, 12, 108
    i) 11, 6, 66    j) 12, 7, 84    k) 4, 11, 44    l) 7, 7, 49
    Answers:
      a) 2 × 7 = 14, 7 × 2 = 14, 14 ÷ 2 = 7, 14 ÷ 7 = 2
      b) 3 × 8 = 24, 8 × 3 = 24, 24 ÷ 3 = 8, 24 ÷ 8 = 3
      c) 4 × 6 = 24, 6 × 4 = 24, 24 ÷ 4 = 6, 24 ÷ 6 = 4
      d) 5 × 9 = 45, 9 × 5 = 45, 45 ÷ 5 = 9, 45 ÷ 9 = 5
      e) 6 × 7 = 42, 7 × 6 = 42, 42 ÷ 6 = 7, 42 ÷ 7 = 6
      f) 7 × 8 = 56, 8 × 7 = 56, 56 ÷ 7 = 8, 56 ÷ 8 = 7
      g) 8 × 9 = 72, 9 × 8 = 72, 72 ÷ 8 = 9, 72 ÷ 9 = 8
      h) 9 × 12 = 108, 12 × 9 = 108, 108 ÷ 9 = 12, 108 ÷ 12 = 9
      i) 11 × 6 = 66, 6 × 11 = 66, 66 ÷ 11 = 6, 66 ÷ 6 = 11
      j) 12 × 7 = 84, 7 × 12 = 84, 84 ÷ 12 = 7, 84 ÷ 7 = 12
      k) 4 × 11 = 44, 11 × 4 = 44, 44 ÷ 4 = 11, 44 ÷ 11 = 4
      l) 7 × 7 = 49, 49 ÷ 7 = 7 (only two facts)

M2. Bigger fact families. Write all the facts.
    a) 25, 4, 100      b) 20, 5, 100      c) 15, 6, 90       d) 12, 12, 144
    e) 50, 2, 100      f) 125, 8, 1,000   g) 13, 3, 39       h) 16, 5, 80
    i) 11, 11, 121     j) 24, 4, 96
    Answers:
      a) 25 × 4 = 100, 4 × 25 = 100, 100 ÷ 25 = 4, 100 ÷ 4 = 25
      b) 20 × 5 = 100, 5 × 20 = 100, 100 ÷ 20 = 5, 100 ÷ 5 = 20
      c) 15 × 6 = 90, 6 × 15 = 90, 90 ÷ 15 = 6, 90 ÷ 6 = 15
      d) 12 × 12 = 144, 144 ÷ 12 = 12 (only two facts)
      e) 50 × 2 = 100, 2 × 50 = 100, 100 ÷ 50 = 2, 100 ÷ 2 = 50
      f) 125 × 8 = 1,000, 8 × 125 = 1,000, 1,000 ÷ 125 = 8, 1,000 ÷ 8 = 125
      g) 13 × 3 = 39, 3 × 13 = 39, 39 ÷ 13 = 3, 39 ÷ 3 = 13
      h) 16 × 5 = 80, 5 × 16 = 80, 80 ÷ 16 = 5, 80 ÷ 5 = 16
      i) 11 × 11 = 121, 121 ÷ 11 = 11 (only two facts)
      j) 24 × 4 = 96, 4 × 24 = 96, 96 ÷ 24 = 4, 96 ÷ 4 = 24

M3. Find the missing factor (first place). Think: divide.
    a) __ × 10 = 40    b) __ × 11 = 88    c) __ × 5 = 40     d) __ × 7 = 21
    e) __ × 5 = 30     f) __ × 9 = 108    g) __ × 2 = 18     h) __ × 7 = 49
    i) __ × 5 = 45     j) __ × 10 = 80    k) __ × 10 = 20    l) __ × 3 = 6
    m) __ × 8 = 96     n) __ × 10 = 110   o) __ × 2 = 10     p) __ × 6 = 48
    q) __ × 9 = 36     r) __ × 4 = 16     s) __ × 5 = 50     t) __ × 8 = 48
    Answers: a) 4   b) 8   c) 8   d) 3   e) 6   f) 12   g) 9   h) 7   i) 9
             j) 8   k) 2   l) 2   m) 12   n) 11   o) 5   p) 8   q) 4   r) 4
             s) 10   t) 6

M4. Find the missing factor (second place).
    a) 4 × __ = 40     b) 5 × __ = 35     c) 3 × __ = 12     d) 10 × __ = 60
    e) 6 × __ = 48     f) 7 × __ = 56     g) 3 × __ = 30     h) 3 × __ = 6
    i) 2 × __ = 20     j) 2 × __ = 8      k) 6 × __ = 54     l) 8 × __ = 48
    m) 12 × __ = 108   n) 5 × __ = 30     o) 11 × __ = 55    p) 10 × __ = 110
    q) 2 × __ = 18     r) 4 × __ = 32     s) 8 × __ = 16     t) 8 × __ = 32
    Answers: a) 10   b) 7   c) 4   d) 6   e) 8   f) 8   g) 10   h) 2   i) 10
             j) 4   k) 9   l) 6   m) 9   n) 6   o) 5   p) 11   q) 9   r) 8
             s) 2   t) 4

M5. Find the missing divisor.
    a) 36 ÷ __ = 9     b) 48 ÷ __ = 4     c) 80 ÷ __ = 10    d) 18 ÷ __ = 6
    e) 44 ÷ __ = 11    f) 20 ÷ __ = 10    g) 144 ÷ __ = 12   h) 110 ÷ __ = 11
    i) 72 ÷ __ = 9     j) 120 ÷ __ = 12   k) 24 ÷ __ = 8     l) 10 ÷ __ = 2
    m) 42 ÷ __ = 7     n) 22 ÷ __ = 11    o) 44 ÷ __ = 4     p) 16 ÷ __ = 2
    q) 84 ÷ __ = 12    r) 33 ÷ __ = 3     s) 15 ÷ __ = 3     t) 30 ÷ __ = 6
    Answers: a) 4   b) 12   c) 8   d) 3   e) 4   f) 2   g) 12   h) 10   i) 8
             j) 10   k) 3   l) 5   m) 6   n) 2   o) 11   p) 8   q) 7   r) 11
             s) 5   t) 5

M6. Find the missing dividend. Think: multiply.
    a) __ ÷ 4 = 11    b) __ ÷ 9 = 8     c) __ ÷ 8 = 11    d) __ ÷ 12 = 2
    e) __ ÷ 9 = 7     f) __ ÷ 11 = 10   g) __ ÷ 2 = 5     h) __ ÷ 6 = 2
    i) __ ÷ 2 = 2     j) __ ÷ 9 = 3     k) __ ÷ 9 = 9     l) __ ÷ 11 = 8
    m) __ ÷ 7 = 7     n) __ ÷ 8 = 10    o) __ ÷ 5 = 6     p) __ ÷ 9 = 5
    q) __ ÷ 12 = 10   r) __ ÷ 4 = 2     s) __ ÷ 3 = 5     t) __ ÷ 5 = 11
    Answers: a) 44   b) 72   c) 88   d) 24   e) 63   f) 110   g) 10   h) 12
             i) 4   j) 27   k) 81   l) 88   m) 49   n) 80   o) 30   p) 45
             q) 120   r) 8   s) 15   t) 55

M7. Use the multiplication to write two division facts. (For a square there is
    only one.)
    a) 14 × 6 = 84       b) 23 × 4 = 92       c) 15 × 7 = 105
    d) 32 × 3 = 96       e) 18 × 5 = 90       f) 25 × 8 = 200
    g) 21 × 9 = 189      h) 16 × 12 = 192     i) 45 × 2 = 90
    j) 19 × 11 = 209     k) 13 × 13 = 169     l) 50 × 20 = 1,000
    m) 36 × 7 = 252      n) 125 × 4 = 500     o) 17 × 6 = 102
    p) 24 × 25 = 600
    Answers:
      a) 84 ÷ 14 = 6, 84 ÷ 6 = 14
      b) 92 ÷ 23 = 4, 92 ÷ 4 = 23
      c) 105 ÷ 15 = 7, 105 ÷ 7 = 15
      d) 96 ÷ 32 = 3, 96 ÷ 3 = 32
      e) 90 ÷ 18 = 5, 90 ÷ 5 = 18
      f) 200 ÷ 25 = 8, 200 ÷ 8 = 25
      g) 189 ÷ 21 = 9, 189 ÷ 9 = 21
      h) 192 ÷ 16 = 12, 192 ÷ 12 = 16
      i) 90 ÷ 45 = 2, 90 ÷ 2 = 45
      j) 209 ÷ 19 = 11, 209 ÷ 11 = 19
      k) 169 ÷ 13 = 13
      l) 1,000 ÷ 50 = 20, 1,000 ÷ 20 = 50
      m) 252 ÷ 36 = 7, 252 ÷ 7 = 36
      n) 500 ÷ 125 = 4, 500 ÷ 4 = 125
      o) 102 ÷ 17 = 6, 102 ÷ 6 = 17
      p) 600 ÷ 24 = 25, 600 ÷ 25 = 24

M8. Find the missing dividend: quotient × divisor + remainder.
    a) __ ÷ 5 = 7 R 3     b) __ ÷ 6 = 8 R 2     c) __ ÷ 4 = 9 R 1
    d) __ ÷ 7 = 6 R 5     e) __ ÷ 3 = 12 R 2    f) __ ÷ 9 = 5 R 8
    g) __ ÷ 4 = 11 R 3    h) __ ÷ 8 = 10 R 7    i) __ ÷ 12 = 4 R 11
    j) __ ÷ 9 = 9 R 4     k) __ ÷ 6 = 15 R 1    l) __ ÷ 7 = 20 R 6
    m) __ ÷ 5 = 13 R 4    n) __ ÷ 4 = 25 R 3    o) __ ÷ 11 = 8 R 10
    p) __ ÷ 3 = 30 R 2
    Answers: a) 38   b) 50   c) 37   d) 47   e) 38   f) 53   g) 47   h) 87
             i) 59   j) 85   k) 91   l) 146   m) 69   n) 103   o) 98   p) 92

M9. Dividing by 1, by the number itself, dividing 0, and dividing by 0. Write
    "no answer" if it cannot be done.
    a) 17 ÷ 1          b) 17 ÷ 17         c) 0 ÷ 17          d) 17 ÷ 0
    e) 250 ÷ 1         f) 250 ÷ 250       g) 0 ÷ 250         h) 1 ÷ 1
    i) 0 ÷ 1           j) 9 ÷ 0           k) 1,000 ÷ 1       l) 1,000 ÷ 1,000
    m) 48 ÷ __ = 48    n) 48 ÷ __ = 1     o) __ ÷ 7 = 0      p) __ ÷ 1 = 36
    q) __ ÷ 36 = 1     r) 0 ÷ __ = 0      s) 0 × 7           t) 7 × 1
    u) 99 ÷ 99         v) 0 ÷ 99          w) 99 ÷ 1          x) 0 ÷ 0
    Answers: a) 17   b) 1   c) 0   d) no answer   e) 250   f) 1   g) 0   h) 1
             i) 0   j) no answer   k) 1,000   l) 1   m) 1   n) 48   o) 0   p) 36
             q) 36   r) any number except 0   s) 0   t) 7   u) 1   v) 0   w) 99
             x) no answer

M10. Check with the inverse. Right or wrong? If wrong, write the correct
     answer.
     a) 84 ÷ 7 = 12     b) 96 ÷ 6 = 17     c) 13 × 6 = 78     d) 117 ÷ 9 = 13
     e) 15 × 8 = 110    f) 132 ÷ 12 = 12   g) 144 ÷ 12 = 12   h) 91 ÷ 7 = 14
     i) 25 × 6 = 150    j) 108 ÷ 4 = 27    k) 76 ÷ 4 = 18     l) 17 × 5 = 85
     m) 150 ÷ 6 = 25    n) 98 ÷ 7 = 13     o) 16 × 9 = 144    p) 119 ÷ 7 = 18
     Answers:
       a) right (12 × 7 = 84)
       b) wrong: 96 ÷ 6 = 16
       c) right (78 ÷ 6 = 13)
       d) right (13 × 9 = 117)
       e) wrong: 15 × 8 = 120
       f) wrong: 132 ÷ 12 = 11
       g) right (12 × 12 = 144)
       h) wrong: 91 ÷ 7 = 13
       i) right (150 ÷ 6 = 25)
       j) right (27 × 4 = 108)
       k) wrong: 76 ÷ 4 = 19
       l) right (85 ÷ 5 = 17)
       m) right (25 × 6 = 150)
       n) wrong: 98 ÷ 7 = 14
       o) right (144 ÷ 9 = 16)
       p) wrong: 119 ÷ 7 = 17

M11. Make both sides equal. Work out the side you can, then use the inverse.
     a) 4 × 6 = 48 ÷ __     b) 3 × __ = 72 ÷ 3     c) 5 × 6 = 60 ÷ __
     d) 2 × __ = 36 ÷ 2     e) 6 × 6 = 72 ÷ __     f) 7 × __ = 84 ÷ 3
     g) 9 × 4 = 72 ÷ __     h) 3 × __ = 60 ÷ 4     i) 8 × 3 = 96 ÷ __
     j) 10 × __ = 100 ÷ 2   k) 12 × 2 = 72 ÷ __    l) 4 × __ = 64 ÷ 4
     Answers: a) 2   b) 8   c) 2   d) 9   e) 2   f) 4   g) 2   h) 5   i) 4
              j) 5   k) 3   l) 4

M12. Missing numbers in every position (mixed).
     a) __ × 9 = 99       b) 132 ÷ __ = 11     c) __ ÷ 8 = 12
     d) 7 × __ = 84       e) 121 ÷ __ = 11     f) __ ÷ 12 = 7
     g) __ × 6 = 72       h) 108 ÷ __ = 9      i) __ ÷ 11 = 11
     j) 8 × __ = 64       k) __ ÷ 7 = 9 R 6    l) 100 ÷ __ = 25
     m) __ × 25 = 200     n) __ ÷ 20 = 15      o) 15 × __ = 90
     p) 144 ÷ __ = 12     q) __ ÷ 9 = 11 R 8   r) __ × 11 = 132
     s) 1,000 ÷ __ = 10   t) __ ÷ 50 = 20      u) 12 × __ = 0
     v) __ ÷ 13 = 1       w) 56 ÷ __ = 8       x) __ × 7 = 56
     Answers: a) 11   b) 12   c) 96   d) 12   e) 11   f) 84   g) 12   h) 12
              i) 121   j) 8   k) 69   l) 4   m) 8   n) 300   o) 6   p) 12
              q) 107   r) 12   s) 100   t) 1,000   u) 0   v) 13   w) 7   x) 8

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: build every fact family with a counter array and turn it round;
  use a triangle card (product at the top, the two factors at the bottom) and
  cover one corner to practise the missing number; always ask "should the
  missing number be bigger or smaller?". Harder: missing numbers with
  remainders, "think of a number" puzzles with 3 steps, and explaining in the
  child's own words why 0 ÷ 5 = 0 but 5 ÷ 0 has no answer.


================================================================================
TOPIC 4.11 — MULTIPLICATION AS SCALING ("3 TIMES AS LONG") AND COMPARISON WORD
             PROBLEMS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - "3 times as many", "3 times as long", "3 times as much" and "3 times as
    heavy" all mean MULTIPLY by 3: 3 times as long as 8 cm is 3 × 8 = 24 cm.
    Multiplying SCALES a number up.
  - "3 times as many" is NOT "3 more": 3 more than 8 is 8 + 3 = 11, but 3
    times 8 is 3 × 8 = 24.
  - "How many times as big?" is answered by DIVIDING: 24 is how many times 8?
    24 ÷ 8 = 3 times.
  - Finding the SMALLER amount: 24 is 3 times some number, so the number is
    24 ÷ 3 = 8.
  - Draw a BAR MODEL: one bar (1 unit) for the smaller amount and 3 equal bars
    (3 units) for the bigger. Then the total is 4 units and the difference is
    2 units.
  - Know the words: twice or double = 2 times; thrice = 3 times; half as much
    = ÷ 2.
  - Scale a recipe: for 3 times as many people, multiply EVERY amount by 3;
    for a third of the people, divide every amount by 3.
  - Compare in two ways: "how many MORE?" (subtract) and "how many TIMES?"
    (divide).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. A red ribbon is 8 cm long. A blue ribbon is 3 times as long. How long is
    the blue ribbon?
        Red:  [  8  ]
        Blue: [  8  ][  8  ][  8  ]   3 × 8 = 24 cm
    Answer: 24 cm. 3 × 8 = 24.
    Teacher explains: Draw the bar model: the red ribbon is one bar of 8 cm;
    the blue ribbon is 3 bars of 8 cm put end to end. "3 times as long" means
    3 lots of the red length: 8 + 8 + 8 = 3 × 8 = 24 cm. Lay 3 real strips of
    8 cm next to a ruler to see it. Now you try: a rope is 7 m long and
    another is 4 times as long. (28 m)

D2. Ravi has 6 marbles. Arjun has 5 times as many marbles as Ravi. How many
    marbles does Arjun have?
        Ravi:  [ 6 ]
        Arjun: [ 6 ][ 6 ][ 6 ][ 6 ][ 6 ]   5 × 6 = 30
    Answer: 30 marbles. 5 × 6 = 30.
    Teacher explains: "5 times as many as Ravi" means 5 groups the size of
    Ravi's group. Draw Ravi's bar with 6 in it, and Arjun's bar as 5 of those:
    5 × 6 = 30. Arjun's amount depends on Ravi's: if Ravi had 7, Arjun would
    have 35. Now you try: Sita has 9 bangles and Gita has 4 times as many.
    (36)

D3. A pen costs ₹12. A school bag costs 8 times as much as the pen. How much
    does the bag cost?
    Answer: ₹96. 8 × 12 = 96.
    Teacher explains: "8 times as much" with money means multiply the price by
    8. 8 × 12: 8 × 10 = 80 and 8 × 2 = 16, so 80 + 16 = 96. The bag costs ₹96.
    Estimate to check: 8 × ₹10 = ₹80, so ₹96 is sensible. Now you try: a toy
    costs ₹15 and a game costs 6 times as much. (₹90)

D4. Meena has 7 stickers. Sita has 3 MORE stickers than Meena. Gita has 3
    TIMES as many stickers as Meena. How many do Sita and Gita have?
        Meena: [   7   ]
        Sita:  [   7   ][3]          7 + 3 = 10
        Gita:  [   7   ][   7   ][   7   ]   3 × 7 = 21
    Answer: Sita has 10 (7 + 3). Gita has 21 (3 × 7).
    Teacher explains: The two questions look alike but mean different things.
    "3 more" adds one small piece: 7 + 3 = 10. "3 times as many" makes three
    whole copies: 7 + 7 + 7 = 21. Draw both bar models: Sita's bar is Meena's
    bar with a little bit (3) stuck on; Gita's bar is 3 Meena-bars. Many
    children add when they should multiply, so ask "MORE or TIMES?" every
    time. Now you try: Meena has 9. How many is 4 more, and 4 times as many?
    (13 and 36)

D5. A tree is 20 m tall and a house is 5 m tall. How many times as tall as the
    house is the tree?
    Answer: 4 times as tall. 20 ÷ 5 = 4.
    Teacher explains: We want to know how many house-heights fit into the
    tree's height. Count in 5s to 20: 5, 10, 15, 20 — four 5s. So 20 ÷ 5 = 4
    and the tree is 4 times as tall. Check by scaling: 4 × 5 = 20. "How many
    times" questions are answered by dividing the bigger amount by the smaller
    one. Now you try: a sack of rice weighs 42 kg and a bag of dal weighs 7
    kg. How many times as heavy is the sack? (6 times)

D6. Papa is 40 years old. His age is 4 times his son's age. How old is the
    son?
        Papa: [ ? ][ ? ][ ? ][ ? ]  = 40
        Son:  [ ? ]                 = 40 ÷ 4 = 10
    Answer: 10 years. 40 ÷ 4 = 10.
    Teacher explains: Here the BIG amount is known and the small one is
    missing. Draw Papa's bar as 4 equal parts making 40; the son's bar is ONE
    of those parts. 4 parts = 40, so 1 part = 40 ÷ 4 = 10. Check: 4 × 10 = 40.
    A common mistake is to multiply (4 × 40 = 160) — ask, "Should the son be
    older or younger than Papa?" Now you try: a train ticket costs ₹45, which
    is 5 times the cost of a bus ticket. How much is the bus ticket? (₹9)

D7. Anu and Bina collect stamps. Bina has 3 times as many as Anu. Together
    they have 48 stamps. How many does each girl have?
        Anu:  [ u ]
        Bina: [ u ][ u ][ u ]   4 units = 48, 1 unit = 12
    Answer: Anu has 12 and Bina has 36.
    Teacher explains: Bar model: Anu is 1 unit, Bina is 3 units, so together
    they are 1 + 3 = 4 units. 4 units = 48, so 1 unit = 48 ÷ 4 = 12. Anu has
    12; Bina has 3 × 12 = 36. Check: 12 + 36 = 48, and 36 is 3 times 12.
    Without the bar model many children do 48 ÷ 3 = 16 — wrong, because the
    TOTAL has 4 units, not 3. Now you try: together 60, and one has 5 times as
    many as the other. (10 and 50)

D8. A goods train is 6 times as long as an engine. The train is 50 m longer
    than the engine. How long is each?
        Engine: [ u ]
        Train:  [ u ][ u ][ u ][ u ][ u ][ u ]
                difference = 5 units = 50 m, 1 unit = 10 m
    Answer: The engine is 10 m long and the train is 60 m long.
    Teacher explains: Bar model: engine 1 unit, train 6 units. The DIFFERENCE
    is 6 − 1 = 5 units, and that is 50 m. So 1 unit = 50 ÷ 5 = 10 m. The
    engine is 10 m and the train is 6 × 10 = 60 m. Check: 60 − 10 = 50 and
    60 = 6 × 10. Now you try: one box has 4 times as many pencils as another,
    and it has 30 more. (10 and 40)

D9. A khichdi recipe for 4 people uses 2 cups of rice, 1 cup of dal and 5 cups
    of water. How much of each is needed for 12 people?
    Answer: 6 cups of rice, 3 cups of dal and 15 cups of water.
    Teacher explains: 12 people is 3 times as many as 4 people (12 ÷ 4 = 3),
    so EVERY amount is multiplied by 3: rice 3 × 2 = 6, dal 3 × 1 = 3, water
    3 × 5 = 15. The scale factor is found first, then used on each line. A
    common mistake is to add 8 cups (because 12 − 4 = 8) — the recipe must
    keep the same TASTE, so all amounts grow by the same number of times. Now
    you try: the same recipe for 8 people. (4 cups rice, 2 cups dal, 10 cups
    water)

D10. A recipe for 12 laddoos uses 6 spoons of ghee and 9 spoons of sugar. How
     much is needed for just 4 laddoos?
     Answer: 2 spoons of ghee and 3 spoons of sugar.
     Teacher explains: 12 laddoos is 3 times as many as 4 (12 ÷ 4 = 3), so for
     4 laddoos we need a THIRD of each amount. Scaling DOWN means dividing
     every amount by 3: ghee 6 ÷ 3 = 2, sugar 9 ÷ 3 = 3. Check by scaling back
     up: 3 × 2 = 6 and 3 × 3 = 9. Now you try: the same recipe for 24 laddoos.
     (12 spoons of ghee, 18 spoons of sugar)

D11. In a cricket match Raju scored 36 runs and Sonu scored 9 runs. (a) How
     many more runs did Raju score? (b) How many times as many runs did Raju
     score?
     Answer: (a) 27 more runs: 36 − 9 = 27. (b) 4 times as many: 36 ÷ 9 = 4.
     Teacher explains: Two ways to compare the same numbers. "How many MORE"
     is the gap between the bars: subtract, 36 − 9 = 27. "How many TIMES" asks
     how many of Sonu's bars fit in Raju's: divide, 36 ÷ 9 = 4. Put both on
     one bar model: Raju's bar is 4 Sonu-bars, and the gap is 3 Sonu-bars,
     3 × 9 = 27. Now you try: 48 runs and 8 runs. (40 more; 6 times as many)

D12. Asha says: "A pencil costs ₹5. A pen costs 4 times as much, so it costs
     ₹9." What is her mistake?
     Answer: She added 4 instead of multiplying by 4. The pen costs
     4 × 5 = ₹20.
     Teacher explains: "Times as much" always means multiply. Asha did
     5 + 4 = 9, which answers "4 rupees MORE". Draw 4 pencil-bars of ₹5:
     5 + 5 + 5 + 5 = 20. Also learn: twice = 2 times, thrice = 3 times, half
     as much = ÷ 2. Now you try: a cat weighs 4 kg and a dog weighs 6 times as
     much. (24 kg)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY

M4. How many times? Divide the bigger number by the smaller.
    a) 45 is __ times 5     b) 33 is __ times 11    c) 36 is __ times 12

M5. Find the smaller number. Divide.
    a) 30 is 2 times __     b) 65 is 5 times __     c) 40 is 5 times __

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: use real strips of paper or Cuisenaire-style rods and lay copies
  end to end for "times as long"; say "MORE or TIMES?" before every question;
  always draw the bar model before calculating. Harder: bar models with three
  people (A has twice B, C has 3 times B, total 72), scaling recipes by
  factors like 1 and a half, and making up their own "times as many" and "more
  than" stories for the same pair of numbers.


================================================================================
TOPIC 4.12 — ORDER OF OPERATIONS (SIMPLE)
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - When a calculation has more than one operation, everybody uses the same
    ORDER. Step 1: BRACKETS first (work out what is inside ( )). Step 2: × and
    ÷, working from LEFT to RIGHT, whichever comes first. Step 3: + and −,
    working from LEFT to RIGHT, whichever comes first.
  - The short names BODMAS (Brackets, Orders, Division, Multiplication,
    Addition, Subtraction) and PEMDAS say the same thing. D and M are partners
    of equal rank, and so are A and S: do not always do × before ÷, or +
    before −. ("Orders", powers such as 3², come later.)
  - Brackets can change the answer: 2 + 3 × 4 = 14 but (2 + 3) × 4 = 20.
  - Common mistakes: 20 − 5 + 3 is 18 (not 12); 24 ÷ 4 × 2 is 12 (not 3);
    30 − 10 ÷ 5 is 28 (not 4).
  - Work one step at a time, rewriting the line each time, and underline the
    part you do first.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Riya buys 3 notebooks at ₹20 each and one pen for ₹10. Her brother writes
    the cost as 10 + 3 × 20. What is the total cost?
    Answer: ₹70. 10 + 3 × 20 = 10 + 60 = 70.
    Teacher explains: The story tells us the order: first find the cost of the
    3 notebooks, 3 × 20 = 60, then add the pen, 60 + 10 = 70. The rule "×
    before +" gives the same answer as the story. If we went left to right,
    (10 + 3) × 20 = 260 — that would be 13 notebooks! Write it step by step:
    10 + 3 × 20 = 10 + 60 = 70. Now you try: 5 + 4 × 6. (29)

D2. Riya had ₹50. She spent ₹12 on a kite and ₹18 on string. How much is left?
    Write ONE calculation with brackets.
    Answer: ₹20. 50 − (12 + 18) = 50 − 30 = 20.
    Teacher explains: She spent 12 + 18 = 30 altogether, and we take the whole
    30 away, so the adding must happen FIRST: put it in brackets.
    50 − (12 + 18) = 50 − 30 = 20. Without brackets, 50 − 12 + 18 means take
    12 away and then ADD 18, which gives 56 — more than she started with!
    Another correct way: 50 − 12 − 18 = 38 − 18 = 20. Now you try:
    40 − (15 + 5). (20)

D3. A bus has 20 passengers. At a stop 5 get off and 3 get on. Sonu writes
    20 − 5 + 3 = 12. What is the right answer?
    Answer: 18. 20 − 5 + 3: first 20 − 5 = 15, then 15 + 3 = 18.
    Teacher explains: + and − have the SAME rank, so we work from left to
    right, just as the bus story happens: first 5 get off (15 left), then 3
    get on (18). Sonu did 5 + 3 = 8 first and then 20 − 8 = 12, as if 8 people
    had got off. Rule: when only + and − are left, go left to right. Now you
    try: 30 − 10 + 4. (24)

D4. 24 laddoos are packed equally into 4 boxes. Amma buys 2 of the boxes.
    Write the calculation and find how many laddoos she buys.
    Answer: 12 laddoos. 24 ÷ 4 × 2 = 6 × 2 = 12.
    Teacher explains: × and ÷ also have the SAME rank, so we go from left to
    right: first 24 ÷ 4 = 6 laddoos in each box, then 6 × 2 = 12 for two
    boxes. A common mistake is to do 4 × 2 = 8 first and then 24 ÷ 8 = 3.
    Remember: "D before M" in BODMAS does NOT mean divide first — it means
    left to right. Now you try: 36 ÷ 6 × 3. (18)

D5. At the stationery shop 4 pens cost ₹5 each and 3 erasers cost ₹6 each.
    Write one calculation for the total and work it out.
    Answer: ₹38. 4 × 5 + 3 × 6 = 20 + 18 = 38.
    Teacher explains: Each group of things is a multiplication, and then the
    two amounts are added. Because × comes before +, we do NOT need brackets:
    4 × 5 + 3 × 6. Step 1: both multiplications, 20 and 18. Step 2: add, 38.
    Underline 4 × 5 and 3 × 6 before starting. Now you try: 2 × 9 + 5 × 4.
    (38)

D6. 2 boys and 3 girls each get 4 balloons. How many balloons is that? Why is
    (2 + 3) × 4 right and 2 + 3 × 4 wrong?
    Answer: 20 balloons. (2 + 3) × 4 = 5 × 4 = 20. Without brackets,
    2 + 3 × 4 = 2 + 12 = 14.
    Teacher explains: First count the children: 2 + 3 = 5; then each gets 4:
    5 × 4 = 20. Because the adding must happen first, it goes in brackets.
    2 + 3 × 4 would mean 2 balloons plus 3 children with 4 each — a different
    story. Brackets are how we tell the reader "do this part first". Now you
    try: (6 + 4) × 5 and 6 + 4 × 5. (50 and 26)

D7. Work out 18 − 12 ÷ 3 × 2 + 5, showing each step.
    Answer: 15.
    Teacher explains: No brackets, so start with × and ÷ from left to right:
    12 ÷ 3 = 4, then 4 × 2 = 8. The line is now 18 − 8 + 5. Then + and − from
    left to right: 18 − 8 = 10, 10 + 5 = 15. Rewrite the line after every
    step: 18 − 12 ÷ 3 × 2 + 5 = 18 − 4 × 2 + 5 = 18 − 8 + 5 = 10 + 5 = 15. Now
    you try: 20 − 8 ÷ 2 × 3 + 1. (9)

D8. Put brackets into 3 + 5 × 2 = 16 to make it true.
    Answer: (3 + 5) × 2 = 16.
    Teacher explains: Without brackets 3 + 5 × 2 = 3 + 10 = 13, not 16. Try
    brackets in each possible place and work it out: (3 + 5) × 2 = 8 × 2 = 16.
    Yes! Brackets round 5 × 2 change nothing, because × is done first anyway.
    Now you try: put brackets into 10 − 4 − 2 = 8. (10 − (4 − 2) = 8)

D9. A bus has 40 seats. 6 rows of 4 seats are already full. Write one
    calculation for the number of empty seats.
    Answer: 40 − 6 × 4 = 40 − 24 = 16 empty seats.
    Teacher explains: The full seats are 6 × 4 = 24, and we subtract them from
    40. Because × is done before −, the calculation 40 − 6 × 4 is already
    right without brackets (we COULD write 40 − (6 × 4), which is the same).
    Doing it left to right would give (40 − 6) × 4 = 136, which is more seats
    than the bus has — always ask "is it sensible?" Now you try: ₹50 − 7 × ₹5.
    (₹15)

D10. True or false? 6 + 4 × 2 = 20.
     Answer: False. 6 + 4 × 2 = 6 + 8 = 14.
     Teacher explains: Do the × first: 4 × 2 = 8, then 6 + 8 = 14. The person
     who wrote 20 added first: 6 + 4 = 10, 10 × 2 = 20. To MAKE it 20 we would
     need brackets: (6 + 4) × 2 = 20. When checking true/false statements,
     work out the left side carefully by the rules and compare. Now you try:
     true or false, 2 × (3 + 4) = 14? (true)

D11. Work out (8 + 4) ÷ (5 − 2).
     Answer: 4. (8 + 4) ÷ (5 − 2) = 12 ÷ 3 = 4.
     Teacher explains: There are two sets of brackets, so work out BOTH before
     anything else: 8 + 4 = 12 and 5 − 2 = 3. Then the line is 12 ÷ 3 = 4.
     Without the brackets, 8 + 4 ÷ 5 − 2 would not even share out exactly!
     Brackets group the numbers so we treat each bracket as a single number.
     Now you try: (15 − 3) ÷ (2 + 2). (3)

D12. Neha works out 30 − 10 ÷ 5 and gets 4. Her friend gets 28. Who is right?
     What would a simple calculator show?
     Answer: The friend: 30 − 10 ÷ 5 = 30 − 2 = 28. Neha did the subtraction
     first.
     Teacher explains: ÷ comes before −, so 10 ÷ 5 = 2 first, then
     30 − 2 = 28. Neha worked left to right: 30 − 10 = 20, 20 ÷ 5 = 4 — that
     would need brackets: (30 − 10) ÷ 5 = 4. Careful: a cheap calculator that
     works out each step as you press the keys will ALSO show 4, because it
     goes left to right. Scientific calculators and computers follow the order
     of operations. Now you try: 40 − 20 ÷ 4. (35)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Only + and −: work from left to right.
    a) 20 − 5 + 3          b) 31 − 3 − 5          c) 52 − 12 − 11
    d) 27 − 9 + 8 − 10     e) 20 − 5 + 24         f) 40 − 19 − 13 + 12
    g) 28 − 23 + 20        h) 54 − 26 − 14 − 9    i) 36 − 17 − 7
    j) 56 − 26 − 12        k) 17 − 2 − 14         l) 45 + 8 − 2 − 16
    m) 52 − 3 + 30 − 9     n) 58 − 13 + 13        o) 60 + 24 − 23 − 9
    p) 40 − 30 + 17        q) 33 − 18 + 16        r) 50 + 24 − 23 + 8
    s) 60 − 5 + 3          t) 27 − 2 − 9 + 18
    Answers: a) 18   b) 23   c) 29   d) 16   e) 39   f) 20   g) 25   h) 5
             i) 12   j) 18   k) 1   l) 35   m) 70   n) 58   o) 52   p) 27
             q) 31   r) 59   s) 58   t) 34

M2. Only × and ÷: work from left to right.

    d) 36 ÷ 9 × 9       e) 20 ÷ 4 × 9       f) 32 ÷ 2 ÷ 4 × 9
    g) 60 ÷ 4 × 8       h) 50 × 2 ÷ 5       i) 7 × 6 × 8 ÷ 8
    j) 54 × 5 ÷ 9       k) 60 × 4 ÷ 4       l) 32 ÷ 8 × 5
    m) 44 ÷ 4 × 8       n) 44 × 5 ÷ 5       o) 30 ÷ 5 × 6
    p) 54 ÷ 9 × 6       q) 32 × 8 ÷ 8       r) 52 × 6 ÷ 4
    s) 33 ÷ 3 × 8       t) 48 × 6 ÷ 2 ÷ 6
    Answers: a) 12   b) 70   c) 63   d) 36   e) 45   f) 36   g) 120   h) 20
             i) 42   j) 30   k) 60   l) 20   m) 88   n) 44   o) 36   p) 36
             q) 32   r) 78   s) 88   t) 24

M3. One + or − and one × or ÷. Do × and ÷ first.
    a) 15 + 28 ÷ 4     b) 25 − 110 ÷ 10   c) 24 + 72 ÷ 8
    d) 20 − 28 ÷ 4     e) 9 + 63 ÷ 9      f) 40 + 9 × 8
    g) 32 − 10 × 2     h) 32 − 40 ÷ 10    i) 6 + 4 × 6
    j) 34 − 5 × 6      k) 33 + 5 × 11     l) 47 − 63 ÷ 9
    m) 36 + 80 ÷ 10    n) 28 − 110 ÷ 10   o) 30 − 6 × 3
    p) 43 − 6 × 4      q) 46 + 21 ÷ 7     r) 19 − 36 ÷ 4
    s) 5 + 5 × 2       t) 27 + 90 ÷ 10
    Answers: a) 22   b) 14   c) 33   d) 13   e) 16   f) 112   g) 12   h) 28
             i) 30   j) 4   k) 88   l) 40   m) 44   n) 17   o) 12   p) 19
             q) 49   r) 10   s) 15   t) 36

M4. Two × or ÷ and one + or −.
    a) 96 ÷ 12 + 8 × 10   b) 40 ÷ 8 × 3 − 8     c) 40 ÷ 4 + 6 ÷ 2
    d) 4 × 7 + 2 × 5      e) 3 × 12 + 3 × 7     f) 8 × 5 − 30 ÷ 10
    g) 96 ÷ 8 + 12 × 6    h) 9 × 11 − 5 × 5     i) 10 × 11 + 4 × 2
    j) 5 × 8 + 4 × 2      k) 9 × 7 − 90 ÷ 10    l) 72 ÷ 8 × 6 − 4
    m) 110 ÷ 10 + 6 × 4   n) 8 + 9 × 8 ÷ 3      o) 77 ÷ 11 + 11 × 7
    p) 8 ÷ 4 + 90 ÷ 9     q) 9 × 10 + 9 × 9     r) 90 ÷ 10 × 9 − 8
    s) 25 ÷ 5 × 3 − 11    t) 10 × 9 + 6 × 6
    Answers: a) 88   b) 7   c) 13   d) 38   e) 57   f) 37   g) 84   h) 74
             i) 118   j) 48   k) 54   l) 50   m) 35   n) 32   o) 84   p) 12
             q) 171   r) 73   s) 4   t) 126

M5. Brackets first.
    a) 31 − (12 + 8)        b) (11 + 12) × 3        c) (10 + 3) × (6 + 1)
    d) (4 + 5) × 3          e) 40 ÷ (11 + 9)        f) 6 × (5 + 4)
    g) (8 + 3) × (5 + 1)    h) (6 + 9) × 8          i) (39 − 30) ÷ 3
    j) 64 ÷ (11 + 5)        k) 56 − (8 + 9)         l) (10 − 7) × 9
    m) (50 + 50) ÷ 5        n) (9 + 12) × (6 + 1)   o) 41 − (14 − 6)
    p) 48 − (8 + 15)        q) (9 + 2) × (2 + 1)    r) (5 + 8) × 5
    s) (7 + 7) × (3 + 1)    t) (4 + 4) × (8 + 1)
    Answers: a) 11   b) 69   c) 91   d) 27   e) 2   f) 54   g) 66   h) 120
             i) 3   j) 4   k) 39   l) 27   m) 20   n) 147   o) 33   p) 25
             q) 33   r) 65   s) 56   t) 72

M6. Work out each pair. Do the brackets change the answer?
    a) 56 − 9 × 6      b) (56 − 9) × 6
    c) 37 − 2 × 4      d) (37 − 2) × 4
    e) 38 − 12 × 2     f) (38 − 12) × 2
    g) 189 ÷ 7 + 2     h) 189 ÷ (7 + 2)
    i) 54 − 6 × 6      j) (54 − 6) × 6
    k) 42 − 10 × 4     l) (42 − 10) × 4
    m) 4 × 5 + 4       n) 4 × (5 + 4)
    o) 49 − 2 × 5      p) (49 − 2) × 5
    q) 12 + 2 × 7      r) (12 + 2) × 7
    s) 273 ÷ 7 + 6     t) 273 ÷ (7 + 6)
    Answers: a) 2   b) 282   c) 29   d) 140   e) 14   f) 52   g) 29   h) 21
             i) 18   j) 288   k) 2   l) 128   m) 24   n) 36   o) 39   p) 235
             q) 26   r) 98   s) 45   t) 21

M7. 3 or 4 operations. Follow the order step by step.
    a) 3 + 14 × 2 − 3          b) (2 + 15 ÷ 15) × 17
    c) 19 × 2 − 10 + 10        d) (13 × 19) − 10 × 16
    e) 13 + 18 − 4 − 15        f) 3 × (2 × 11 + 12) + 5
    g) (10 + 10 − 9) × 15      h) (9 + 14) − 14 + 11
    i) (9 + 14 − 15) × 2       j) 2 + 13 + 6 × 13
    k) 7 + 20 − 2 − 3 − 6      l) 7 + 11 − 14 + 14
    m) 6 + 17 − 9 + 5          n) 11 + (11 × 7) − 9
    o) 16 × 8 ÷ 16 × 9 − 3     p) 10 ÷ 5 + 14 + 2
    q) 14 − 8 + 19 − 15        r) 13 − 17 ÷ 17 × 3
    s) 3 × 10 − 8 − 3          t) (9 × 11 − 8 − 19) − 5
    Answers: a) 28   b) 51   c) 38   d) 87   e) 12   f) 107   g) 165   h) 20
             i) 16   j) 93   k) 16   l) 18   m) 19   n) 79   o) 69   p) 18
             q) 10   r) 10   s) 19   t) 67

M8. Put in ONE pair of brackets to make each statement true.
    a) 10 × 4 + 8 = 120       b) 4 + 2 × 4 = 24
    c) 7 + 3 × 6 = 60         d) 12 − 8 × 5 = 20
    e) 10 + 4 ÷ 7 = 2         f) 11 + 4 × 6 = 90
    g) 2 + 4 ÷ 6 = 1          h) 7 + 3 × 8 = 80
    i) 9 − 3 + 3 = 3          j) 9 + 3 × 10 = 120
    k) 11 × 2 − 11 + 4 = 7    l) 2 × 8 − 11 ÷ 11 = 14
    m) 10 + 2 − 7 × 2 = 10    n) 10 × 3 + 8 − 11 = 99
    o) 7 − 11 − 6 + 9 = 11    p) 2 × 11 + 10 − 9 = 24
    Answers:
      a) 10 × (4 + 8)
      b) (4 + 2) × 4
      c) (7 + 3) × 6
      d) (12 − 8) × 5
      e) (10 + 4) ÷ 7
      f) (11 + 4) × 6
      g) (2 + 4) ÷ 6
      h) (7 + 3) × 8
      i) 9 − (3 + 3)
      j) (9 + 3) × 10
      k) 11 × 2 − (11 + 4)
      l) 2 × (8 − 11 ÷ 11)
      m) (10 + 2 − 7) × 2
      n) 10 × (3 + 8) − 11
      o) 7 − (11 − 6) + 9
      p) 2 × (11 + 10 − 9)

M9. True or false? If false, write the correct value.
    a) 19 × 18 ÷ 19 + 12 − 4 = 26   b) 7 × (14 − 8) + 5 × 6 = 72
    c) 8 × (15 + 5 − 16) = 32       d) 8 + 12 × 3 + 6 − 12 = 54
    e) 4 + 19 + 11 + 7 = 41         f) 3 + 14 + 14 × 13 = 403
    g) 15 ÷ 5 + 13 × 3 = 48         h) 11 + 4 × 6 − 2 = 88
    i) 11 + 9 × 7 + 8 = 148         j) 10 + 14 − 4 − 13 = 7
    k) 14 − 8 + 13 − 19 + 4 = 4     l) 17 × 2 + 5 × 18 = 702
    m) 15 − 2 + 3 × 15 − 4 = 236    n) 4 × 10 − 7 × 5 = 165
    o) 7 + 4 × 8 + 17 = 56          p) 9 × 4 + 20 ÷ 4 = 14
    q) 15 + 13 + 3 × 11 = 61        r) (7 + 16 − 15) × 3 = 24
    s) 17 − 11 − 2 + 18 = 22        t) 16 + 2 + 10 × 15 = 420
    Answers: a) true   b) true   c) true   d) false (38)   e) true
             f) false (199)   g) false (42)   h) false (33)   i) false (82)
             j) true   k) true   l) false (124)   m) false (54)   n) false (5)
             o) true   p) false (41)   q) true   r) true   s) true
             t) false (168)

M10. Write +, −, × or ÷ in each gap to make it true (each has only one
     answer).
     a) 3 __ 6 __ 7 = 2       b) 11 __ 7 __ 2 = 6
     c) 5 __ 2 __ 11 = 27     d) 11 __ 4 __ 4 = 12
     e) 11 __ 4 __ 4 = 27     f) 3 __ 5 __ 4 = 11
     g) 7 __ 10 __ 2 = 15     h) 4 __ 10 __ 5 = 9
     i) 10 __ 5 __ 11 = 4     j) 8 __ 5 __ 10 = 58
     k) 7 __ 5 __ 6 = 37      l) 7 __ 10 __ 4 = 47

     Answers:

       l) 7 + 10 × 4

       n) 8 + 11 − 9

M11. Find the missing number. Remember the order of operations.
     a) 6 + __ × 9 = 87     b) __ × 6 − 11 = 31
     c) 3 + 3 × __ = 33     d) (7 + __) × 3 = 27

              j) 9   k) 4   l) 3   m) 4   n) 6   o) 30   p) 9

M12. Mixed revision (harder). Show each step.
     a) (2 + 7) × (7 + 2) ÷ 9    b) (9 + 10) × 8 − 4 × 9
     c) (8 + 10) × 3 − 4 × 6     d) 2 × 12 + 5 × 7 − 11
     e) 4 × 10 − 3 × 2 + 12      f) (133 − 7 × 8) ÷ 11
     g) 8 × 8 − 3 × 9 + 2        h) 63 − 3 × 10 + 60 ÷ 12
     i) 7 × 9 − 11 × 4 + 10      j) 6 × 6 + 12 × 8 − 9

     o) 10 × 7 + 12 × 9 − 12     p) 4 × (8 + 5 × 3) − 3

              q) 61   r) 124   s) 86   t) 208

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: use only two operations at a time; underline or circle the part
  to do first and rewrite the whole line after each step; tell a shopping
  story for each expression so the order makes sense. Harder: 4-5 operation
  expressions with two sets of brackets, making a target number from four
  given numbers (like the "make 24" game with 4, 6, 8, 3), and writing one
  expression for a two-step word problem.


================================================================================
TOPIC 4.13 — FACTORS AND MULTIPLES
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - A FACTOR of a number divides it exactly, with no remainder. The factors of
    12 are 1, 2, 3, 4, 6 and 12.
  - Factors come in FACTOR PAIRS that multiply to the number: 1 × 12, 2 × 6,
    3 × 4. Find them by trying 1, 2, 3, ... in order and stop when the pairs
    meet.
  - Every number has 1 and itself as factors. A factor is never bigger than
    the number. A square number (36 = 6 × 6) has an ODD number of factors.
  - A MULTIPLE of a number is in its times table: the multiples of 6 are 6,
    12, 18, 24, ... and they go on for ever.
  - "4 is a factor of 20" and "20 is a multiple of 4" say the same thing,
    because 4 × 5 = 20.
  - COMMON factors are shared by two numbers; the biggest is the HIGHEST
    COMMON FACTOR (HCF). Common factors of 12 and 18: 1, 2, 3, 6; HCF = 6.
  - COMMON multiples are in both times tables; the smallest is the LOWEST
    COMMON MULTIPLE (LCM). Multiples of 4 and 6 in common: 12, 24, 36, ...;
    LCM = 12.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Arjun has 12 chairs to set out in a rectangle, with the same number in
    every row. What rectangles can he make? What are the factors of 12?
        1 × 12:  ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ●
        2 × 6:   ● ● ● ● ● ●     3 × 4:  ● ● ● ●
                 ● ● ● ● ● ●             ● ● ● ●
                                         ● ● ● ●
    Answer: 1 row of 12, 2 rows of 6, 3 rows of 4 (and 4 rows of 3, 6 rows of
    2, 12 rows of 1). The factors of 12 are 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 12.
    Teacher explains: Each rectangle is a multiplication that makes 12:
    1 × 12, 2 × 6, 3 × 4. The numbers in these facts are the FACTORS of 12.
    Try every number of rows: 5 rows? 12 ÷ 5 = 2 R 2, the rows are not equal,
    so 5 is not a factor. Draw each rectangle on squared paper. Now you try:
    18 chairs. (factors 1, 2, 3, 6, 9, 18)

D2. Find all the factors of 36 in an organised way.
        1 × 36
        2 × 18
        3 × 12
        4 × 9
        6 × 6   <- the pairs meet
    Answer: 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 9, 12, 18, 36 (9 factors).
    Teacher explains: Write the pairs in order, starting at 1: 1 × 36, 2 × 18,
    3 × 12, 4 × 9, (5? 36 ÷ 5 = 7 R 1, no), 6 × 6. The next number to try is
    7, but 7 is more than 6, and the pairs have MET at 6 × 6, so we stop — we
    have found them all. Read the first numbers down and the second numbers
    back up: 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 9, 12, 18, 36. 6 is only written once, so a square
    number has an ODD number of factors. Now you try: 48. (1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8,
    12, 16, 24, 48)

D3. Is 7 a factor of 91?
    Answer: Yes. 91 ÷ 7 = 13 with no remainder, because 7 × 13 = 91.
    Teacher explains: To test whether a number is a factor, divide and look at
    the remainder: remainder 0 means YES, any other remainder means NO. 91
    does not look like a 7-table number, but 70 + 21 = 91, which is 10 sevens
    + 3 sevens = 13 sevens. So 7 and 13 are a factor pair of 91. Now you try:
    is 6 a factor of 80? (No: 80 ÷ 6 = 13 R 2)

D4. Rahul saves ₹8 every week. Write how much he has saved after each of the
    first 10 weeks. What are these numbers called?
    Answer: ₹8, ₹16, ₹24, ₹32, ₹40, ₹48, ₹56, ₹64, ₹72, ₹80. They are the
    MULTIPLES of 8.
    Teacher explains: Each week adds another 8, so we are counting in 8s:
    these are the numbers in the 8 times table, 1 × 8, 2 × 8, 3 × 8 ...
    Multiples never stop: week 100 gives 800. Notice that 8 itself is the
    first multiple of 8, and every multiple of 8 is at least 8 (except 0). Now
    you try: the first 10 multiples of 7. (7, 14, 21, 28, 35, 42, 49, 56, 63,
    70)

D5. Is 84 a multiple of 6? Is 95 a multiple of 4?
    Answer: 84 is a multiple of 6 (6 × 14 = 84). 95 is not a multiple of 4
    (95 ÷ 4 = 23 R 3).
    Teacher explains: "Is 84 a multiple of 6?" is the same question as "Is 6 a
    factor of 84?" Divide: 84 ÷ 6 = 14 exactly, so yes. 95 ÷ 4 = 23 R 3, a
    remainder, so no. A quick check for 4: every multiple of 4 is even, and 95
    is odd, so it cannot be one. Now you try: is 72 a multiple of 8? (Yes,
    9 × 8 = 72)

D6. Meena has 12 pencils and 18 erasers. She wants to make gift bags that are
    all exactly the same, using ALL the pencils and erasers. What is the
    greatest number of bags she can make?
    Answer: 6 bags, each with 2 pencils and 3 erasers. 6 is the HCF of 12 and
    18.
    Teacher explains: The number of bags must divide 12 (so the pencils share
    equally) AND divide 18 (so the erasers share equally): it must be a COMMON
    FACTOR. Factors of 12: 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 12. Factors of 18: 1, 2, 3, 6, 9,
    18. Common factors: 1, 2, 3, 6. The greatest is 6, the highest common
    factor. Each bag gets 12 ÷ 6 = 2 pencils and 18 ÷ 6 = 3 erasers. Now you
    try: 16 laddoos and 24 barfis. (8 boxes, each with 2 laddoos and 3 barfis)

D7. At the temple one bell rings every 4 minutes and another every 6 minutes.
    They ring together at 9:00. When do they next ring together?
    Answer: At 9:12. 12 is the LCM of 4 and 6.
    Teacher explains: List the minutes after 9:00 when each bell rings. First
    bell: 4, 8, 12, 16, 20, 24 (multiples of 4). Second bell: 6, 12, 18, 24
    (multiples of 6). The COMMON multiples are 12, 24, ... The smallest is 12,
    the lowest common multiple, so they ring together 12 minutes later, at
    9:12, and then again at 9:24. Now you try: two lights flash every 3 and
    every 5 seconds. After how many seconds do they flash together? (15
    seconds)

D8. Why does 16 have an odd number of factors, but 18 has an even number?
    Answer: Factors of 16: 1, 2, 4, 8, 16 (5 factors, odd). Factors of 18: 1,
    2, 3, 6, 9, 18 (6 factors, even). 16 is a square number, 4 × 4.
    Teacher explains: Factors usually come in PAIRS, which gives an even
    number. For 18: 1 × 18, 2 × 9, 3 × 6 — three pairs, 6 factors. For 16:
    1 × 16, 2 × 8, 4 × 4. The last pair uses the SAME number twice, so 4 is
    counted only once: 5 factors. Only square numbers (1, 4, 9, 16, 25, 36,
    ...) have an odd number of factors. Now you try: the factors of 25. (1, 5,
    25)

D9. Riddle: I am a multiple of 5 and a factor of 60. I am odd and bigger than
    10. Who am I?
    Answer: 15.
    Teacher explains: Make a list and cross out. Factors of 60: 1, 2, 3, 4, 5,
    6, 10, 12, 15, 20, 30, 60. Multiples of 5 in that list: 5, 10, 15, 20, 30,
    60. Odd ones: 5, 15. Bigger than 10: 15. Check: 15 = 3 × 5 and
    60 ÷ 15 = 4. Riddles like this make children use both words correctly. Now
    you try: I am a multiple of 4 and a factor of 48, and I am between 13 and
    20. (16)

D10. 24 children are to be put into equal teams, with at least 2 teams and at
     least 2 children in each team. In how many ways can it be done?
     Answer: 6 ways: 2 teams of 12, 3 of 8, 4 of 6, 6 of 4, 8 of 3, 12 of 2.
     Teacher explains: The number of teams must be a factor of 24: 1, 2, 3, 4,
     6, 8, 12, 24. We cannot have 1 team (not "at least 2 teams") or 24 teams
     (only 1 child in each), so the choices are 2, 3, 4, 6, 8 and 12 teams: 6
     ways. Factors help whenever things must be shared or grouped with none
     left over. Now you try: 30 children. (6 ways: 2, 3, 5, 6, 10 or 15 teams)

D11. Samosas come in packs of 6 and chutney cups in packs of 8. Papa wants
     exactly the same number of samosas and chutney cups. What is the smallest
     number he can buy of each, and how many packs is that?
     Answer: 24 of each: 4 packs of samosas and 3 packs of chutney cups. 24 is
     the LCM of 6 and 8.
     Teacher explains: The number of samosas must be a multiple of 6: 6, 12,
     18, 24, 30, ... The number of chutney cups must be a multiple of 8: 8,
     16, 24, 32, ... The first number in BOTH lists is 24. So he buys
     24 ÷ 6 = 4 packs of samosas and 24 ÷ 8 = 3 packs of chutney. A common
     mistake is to multiply 6 × 8 = 48; that works too but is not the
     SMALLEST. Now you try: plates come in packs of 10 and cups in packs of 4.
     (20 of each)

D12. Karan says, "The factors of 20 are 20, 40, 60, 80." What is his mistake?
     Answer: He wrote MULTIPLES of 20. The factors of 20 are 1, 2, 4, 5, 10,
     20.
     Teacher explains: Factors are SMALL: they fit into the number (they
     divide it). Multiples are BIG: the number fits into them (they are in its
     table). A memory trick: "Factors Fit in, Multiples are Many." Every
     number has only a few factors, but multiples go on for ever. 20 is both a
     factor and a multiple of itself. Now you try: write 4 multiples and all
     the factors of 15. (15, 30, 45, 60; and 1, 3, 5, 15)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------

    a) 1    b) 2    c) 3    d) 4    e) 5    f) 6    g) 7    h) 8    i) 9

M4. Write all the factor pairs (numbers to 100).
    a) 60    b) 72    c) 84    d) 90    e) 96    f) 100   g) 64

M6. Factor or not? Divide and look at the remainder.
    a) 4 factor of 14?    b) 10 factor of 94?   c) 10 factor of 80?
    d) 5 factor of 95?    e) 11 factor of 62?   f) 10 factor of 10?
    g) 5 factor of 84?    h) 8 factor of 16?    i) 4 factor of 13?
    j) 10 factor of 20?   k) 2 factor of 86?    l) 6 factor of 81?
    m) 5 factor of 73?    n) 5 factor of 47?    o) 7 factor of 84?
    p) 12 factor of 63?   q) 4 factor of 35?    r) 6 factor of 48?
    s) 9 factor of 70?    t) 8 factor of 96?    u) 2 factor of 66?
    v) 3 factor of 27?    w) 9 factor of 29?    x) 6 factor of 42?
    Answers: a) no (3 R 2)   b) no (9 R 4)   c) yes (10 × 8)   d) yes (5 × 19)
             e) no (5 R 7)   f) yes (10 × 1)   g) no (16 R 4)   h) yes (8 × 2)
             i) no (3 R 1)   j) yes (10 × 2)   k) yes (2 × 43)   l) no (13 R 3)
             m) no (14 R 3)   n) no (9 R 2)   o) yes (7 × 12)   p) no (5 R 3)
             q) no (8 R 3)   r) yes (6 × 8)   s) no (7 R 7)   t) yes (8 × 12)
             u) yes (2 × 33)   v) yes (3 × 9)   w) no (3 R 2)   x) yes (6 × 7)

M7. Multiple or not? ("48 multiple of 6" means: is 48 a multiple of 6?)
    a) 117 multiple of 2    b) 120 multiple of 4    c) 35 multiple of 2
    d) 108 multiple of 6    e) 62 multiple of 4     f) 118 multiple of 11
    g) 88 multiple of 5     h) 20 multiple of 4     i) 35 multiple of 8
    j) 24 multiple of 12    k) 88 multiple of 8     l) 115 multiple of 5
    m) 99 multiple of 3     n) 28 multiple of 8     o) 90 multiple of 10

             e) no (15 R 2)   f) no (10 R 8)   g) no (17 R 3)   h) yes (4 × 5)
             i) no (4 R 3)   j) yes (12 × 2)   k) yes (8 × 11)   l) yes (5 × 23)
             m) yes (3 × 33)   n) no (3 R 4)   o) yes (10 × 9)   p) yes (3 × 35)
             q) no (10 R 1)   r) no (39 R 2)   s) yes (6 × 15)   t) yes (2 × 40)
             u) yes (5 × 12)   v) no (3 R 3)   w) no (14 R 2)   x) no (9 R 6)

M8. List the common factors and circle the highest common factor (HCF).
    a) 12 and 18   b) 8 and 12    c) 15 and 20   d) 16 and 24
    e) 9 and 12    f) 10 and 25   g) 14 and 21   h) 18 and 27
    i) 24 and 36   j) 20 and 30   k) 7 and 11    l) 30 and 45
    Answers:
