    s) MMMCMXXXVI   t) MMCMXLIX
    Answers: a) 1460   b) 1371   c) 3999   d) 1271   e) 3613   f) 3819   g) 3949
             h) 1603   i) 2222   j) 3623   k) 3499   l) 3119   m) 1444   n) 1990
             o) 3661   p) 3625   q) 2947   r) 2696   s) 3936   t) 2949

M10. Is it a correct Roman numeral? If yes, write its value. If not, write the
     number it was trying to show and the correct numeral.
     a) XIX      b) DD       c) IM       d) XIV      e) VL
     f) IIII     g) VV       h) XXXX     i) CCCC     j) XCIX
     k) XM       l) IL       m) MCMXC    n) IC       o) CDXLIV
     p) XLIX     q) XD       r) LL       s) LXX      t) VIIII
     Answers: a) yes, 19   b) no; 1000 = M   c) no; 999 = CMXCIX   d) yes, 14
              e) no; 45 = XLV   f) no; 4 = IV   g) no; 10 = X   h) no; 40 = XL
              i) no; 400 = CD   j) yes, 99   k) no; 990 = CMXC
              l) no; 49 = XLIX   m) yes, 1990   n) no; 99 = XCIX   o) yes, 444
              p) yes, 49   q) no; 490 = CDXC   r) no; 100 = C   s) yes, 70
              t) no; 9 = IX

M11. A clock face has Roman numerals. Write the time in ordinary numbers (like
     4:00 or 7:30).
     a) hour hand at IV, minute hand at XII
     b) hour hand halfway between VII and VIII, minute hand at VI
     c) hour hand just after IX, minute hand at III
     d) hour hand between XI and XII, nearer XII, minute hand at IX
     e) hour hand at II, minute hand at XII
     f) hour hand at XII, minute hand at XII
     g) hour hand halfway between III and IV, minute hand at VI
     h) hour hand between VI and VII, nearer VII, minute hand at IX
     i) hour hand just after X, minute hand at II
     j) hour hand just after VIII, minute hand at IV
     k) hour hand between I and II, nearer II, minute hand at VIII
     l) hour hand between V and VI, nearer VI, minute hand at X
     Answers: a) 4:00   b) 7:30   c) 9:15   d) 11:45   e) 2:00   f) 12:00
              g) 3:30   h) 6:45   i) 10:10   j) 8:20   k) 1:40   l) 5:50

M12. Work out a) to h) and write the answer in Roman numerals. For i) to p)
     write <, > or =.
     a) XIV + XXVI = __          b) L − XIII = __
     c) XXIV + XVIII = __        d) C − I = __
     e) XII × III = __           f) L ÷ V = __
     g) MMXXVI − MCMXLVII = __   h) CD + CD = __
     i) XC __ CX                 j) XLIX __ LI
     k) MCM __ MM                l) IX __ XI
     m) CDXC __ CDIX             n) XL __ LX
     o) MMXXVI __ MMXXIV         p) DCCC __ CM
     Answers:
       a) 14 + 26 = 40 = XL
       b) 50 − 13 = 37 = XXXVII
       c) 24 + 18 = 42 = XLII
       d) 100 − 1 = 99 = XCIX
       e) 12 × 3 = 36 = XXXVI
       f) 50 ÷ 5 = 10 = X
       g) 2026 − 1947 = 79 = LXXIX
       h) 400 + 400 = 800 = DCCC
       i) XC < CX (90 < 110)
       j) XLIX < LI (49 < 51)
       k) MCM < MM (1900 < 2000)
       l) IX < XI (9 < 11)
       m) CDXC > CDIX (490 > 409)
       n) XL < LX (40 < 60)
       o) MMXXVI > MMXXIV (2026 > 2024)
       p) DCCC < CM (800 < 900)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: make cards for I, V, X, L, C, D, M and build numbers with them;
  first master 1 to 12 on a clock face, then 1 to 50; always split the number by
  place value before writing (47 = 40 + 7 = XL + VII) and underline subtraction
  pairs before reading. Harder: convert years of famous events and film credits,
  find the longest numeral below 100 (LXXXVIII), explain why IIII, IC and VX are
  wrong, and write their own age, birth year and today's date in Roman numerals.


================================================================================
TOPIC 4.28 — PERIMETER OF POLYGONS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - PERIMETER is the total length of the boundary of a closed shape: the
    distance all the way round. Add the lengths of ALL the sides.
  - Square: 4 equal sides, P = 4 × side. Rectangle: opposite sides are equal, P
    = l + b + l + b = 2 × (l + b). Equilateral triangle: P = 3 × side.
  - A REGULAR polygon has all sides equal: P = number of sides × side (pentagon
    5, hexagon 6, octagon 8, decagon 10).
  - An IRREGULAR polygon: add every side. Keep a tick on each side as you add it
    so none is missed or counted twice.
  - Find a missing side from the perimeter: square side = P ÷ 4; rectangle: the
    other side = P ÷ 2 − the known side; any shape: missing side = P − (sum of
    the other sides).
  - Rectilinear (L-shaped) figures: find missing sides from the opposite sides
    first (the lengths going across on top add up to the length across the
    bottom).
  - On a grid, count the unit edges round the outside.
  - Perimeter is a LENGTH, so it is measured in mm, cm, m or km. Change to the
    same unit before adding: 1 m = 100 cm.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. A rectangular garden is 12 m long and 8 m wide. How much fencing is needed
    to go all the way round it? If fencing costs ₹25 per metre, what is the
    cost?
                  12 m
           +----------------+
           |                |
        8 m|                |8 m
           |                |
           +----------------+
                  12 m
    Answer: 40 m of fencing, costing ₹1000. P = 2 × (12 + 8) = 40 m;
    40 × 25 = 1000.
    Teacher explains: Walk round the garden in your mind: 12 m, then 8 m, then
    12 m, then 8 m. Add: 12 + 8 + 12 + 8 = 40 m. Because opposite sides are
    equal, we can add one length and one breadth and double:
    2 × (12 + 8) = 2 × 20 = 40 m. Or 2 × 12 + 2 × 8 = 24 + 16 = 40. Cost: 40 m ×
    ₹25 = ₹1000. Now you try: a 15 m by 10 m field. (50 m)

D2. Riya puts lace round a square photo frame of side 15 cm. How much lace does
    she need?
    Answer: 60 cm. P = 4 × 15 = 60 cm.
    Teacher explains: A square has 4 equal sides, so instead of
    15 + 15 + 15 + 15 we multiply: 4 × 15 = 60 cm. Remember the unit: the answer
    is a length, 60 cm. Now you try: a square tile of side 25 cm. (100 cm)

D3. A triangular park has sides 5 m, 7 m and 9 m. What is its perimeter? What is
    the perimeter of an equilateral triangle with sides of 6 cm?
    Answer: 5 + 7 + 9 = 21 m. The equilateral triangle: 3 × 6 = 18 cm.
    Teacher explains: For any triangle, add the three sides: 5 + 7 + 9 = 21 m.
    An equilateral triangle has three EQUAL sides, so multiply: 3 × 6 = 18 cm.
    Check: 6 + 6 + 6 = 18. Now you try: a triangle with sides 12 cm, 9 cm and
    15 cm. (36 cm)

D4. Honeycomb cells are regular hexagons. If each side is 4 mm, what is the
    perimeter of one cell? What is the perimeter of a regular pentagon with
    sides of 7 cm?
    Answer: Hexagon: 6 × 4 = 24 mm. Pentagon: 5 × 7 = 35 cm.
    Teacher explains: "Regular" means all sides are equal. A hexagon has 6
    sides, so P = 6 × 4 = 24 mm. A pentagon has 5 sides, so P = 5 × 7 = 35 cm.
    The rule for any regular polygon is: number of sides × length of one side.
    Count the sides carefully on a drawing first. Now you try: a regular octagon
    of side 3 cm. (24 cm)

D5. A farmer's field has five straight sides of 12 m, 15 m, 9 m, 11 m and 13 m.
    How long is the path all round it?
    Answer: 60 m. 12 + 15 + 9 + 11 + 13 = 60.
    Teacher explains: The sides are all different, so there is no short cut: add
    all five. Tick each side on the drawing as you add it. Look for easy pairs:
    9 + 11 = 20 and 12 + 13 + 15 = 40, so the total is 20 + 40 = 60 m. A common
    mistake is to leave out one side. Now you try: sides 8 cm, 6 cm, 10 cm,
    7 cm. (31 cm)

D6. The perimeter of a square is 36 cm. How long is each side?
    Answer: 9 cm. 36 ÷ 4 = 9.
    Teacher explains: A square has 4 equal sides, and together they make 36 cm.
    Share 36 into 4 equal parts: 36 ÷ 4 = 9 cm. Check: 4 × 9 = 36. Finding the
    side is the opposite of finding the perimeter, so we divide instead of
    multiply. Now you try: a square of perimeter 100 m. (25 m)

D7. A rectangle has a perimeter of 30 cm. Its length is 9 cm. What is its
    breadth?
    Answer: 6 cm. 30 ÷ 2 = 15, and 15 − 9 = 6.
    Teacher explains: The perimeter is 2 lengths and 2 breadths. Half of the
    perimeter is 1 length + 1 breadth: 30 ÷ 2 = 15 cm. The length is 9, so the
    breadth is 15 − 9 = 6 cm. Check: 2 × (9 + 6) = 30. A common mistake is
    30 − 9 = 21, forgetting that there are two lengths. Now you try: perimeter
    50 m, breadth 10 m. (length 15 m)

D8. A triangle has a perimeter of 25 cm. Two sides are 8 cm and 10 cm. How long
    is the third side?
    Answer: 7 cm. 25 − (8 + 10) = 25 − 18 = 7.
    Teacher explains: The three sides add up to 25. The two we know add up to
    8 + 10 = 18. The missing side is what is left: 25 − 18 = 7 cm. Check:
    8 + 10 + 7 = 25. The same idea works for any polygon: missing side =
    perimeter − the sum of the other sides. Now you try: a quadrilateral with
    perimeter 40 cm and sides 12, 9 and 11 cm. (8 cm)

D9. Find the missing sides p and q of this L-shaped room, and then its
    perimeter. (All corners are right angles.)
                      6 m
               +------------+
               |            | 3 m     p
           8 m |            +--------+
               |                     |
               |                     | q
               |                     |
               +---------------------+
                         10 m
    Answer: p = 10 − 6 = 4 m, q = 8 − 3 = 5 m. Perimeter =
    6 + 3 + 4 + 5 + 10 + 8 = 36 m.
    Teacher explains: Look at the lengths going ACROSS: the bottom is 10 m, and
    the top is made of two parts, 6 m and p. So 6 + p = 10, and p = 4 m. Now the
    lengths going UP and DOWN: the left side is 8 m, and the right side is made
    of two parts, 3 m and q. So 3 + q = 8, and q = 5 m. Now add all six sides:
    6 + 3 + 4 + 5 + 10 + 8 = 36 m. Notice that 2 × (10 + 8) = 36 too: an L-shape
    has the same perimeter as the rectangle round it. Now you try: top 5 m, drop
    2 m, bottom 9 m, left 6 m. (p = 4, q = 4, P = 30 m)

D10. Each small square on the grid has sides of 1 cm. What is the perimeter of
     this shape?
         +--+--+--+
         |        |
         +        +
         |        |
         +  +--+--+
         |  |
         +  +
         |  |
         +--+
     Answer: 14 cm. Count the unit edges all round the outside.
     Teacher explains: Put your finger on a corner and walk round the outside,
     counting each 1 cm edge: across the top 3, down the right 2, back to the
     left 2, down 2, across the bottom 1, and up the left side 4.
     3 + 2 + 2 + 2 + 1 + 4 = 14 cm. Mark the corner where you started so you
     know when to stop. Do not count the lines inside the shape. Now you try: a
     3 by 3 square on the grid. (12 cm)

D11. A table top is 2 m long and 75 cm wide. What is its perimeter in
     centimetres, and in metres?
     Answer: 550 cm = 5 m 50 cm = 5.5 m. 2 × (200 + 75) = 550.
     Teacher explains: The sides are in different units, so change them to the
     same unit first: 2 m = 200 cm. Then P = 2 × (200 + 75) = 2 × 275 = 550 cm.
     Since 100 cm = 1 m, 550 cm = 5 m 50 cm = 5.5 m. Adding 2 + 75 = 77 would be
     a big mistake. Now you try: a rectangle 1 m by 40 cm. (280 cm = 2.8 m)

D12. Ravi says a rectangle 6 cm by 4 cm has a perimeter of 10 cm. Meena says it
     is 24 cm. Who is right?
     Answer: Neither. P = 2 × (6 + 4) = 20 cm.
     Teacher explains: Ravi added only two sides, 6 + 4 = 10: that is only half
     the way round. Meena multiplied, 6 × 4 = 24: that is the AREA (the number
     of 1 cm squares inside), not the perimeter. The perimeter is
     6 + 4 + 6 + 4 = 20 cm. Draw it on squared paper and walk round it with a
     finger. Now you try: a rectangle 7 cm by 3 cm. (20 cm)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Find the perimeter of each square. (P = 4 × side.)
    a) side 1 cm    b) side 3 cm    c) side 5 cm    d) side 7 cm
    e) side 8 cm    f) side 9 cm    g) side 10 cm   h) side 12 cm
    i) side 15 cm   j) side 20 cm   k) side 25 cm   l) side 11 m
    m) side 30 m    n) side 45 m    o) side 100 m   p) side 50 m
    Answers: a) 4 × 1 = 4 cm   b) 4 × 3 = 12 cm   c) 4 × 5 = 20 cm
             d) 4 × 7 = 28 cm   e) 4 × 8 = 32 cm   f) 4 × 9 = 36 cm
             g) 4 × 10 = 40 cm   h) 4 × 12 = 48 cm   i) 4 × 15 = 60 cm
             j) 4 × 20 = 80 cm   k) 4 × 25 = 100 cm   l) 4 × 11 = 44 m
             m) 4 × 30 = 120 m   n) 4 × 45 = 180 m   o) 4 × 100 = 400 m
             p) 4 × 50 = 200 m

M2. Find the perimeter of each rectangle. (P = 2 × (l + b).)
    a) l = 9 cm, b = 4 cm     b) l = 6 cm, b = 3 cm     c) l = 10 cm, b = 5 cm
    d) l = 8 cm, b = 2 cm     e) l = 12 cm, b = 7 cm    f) l = 15 cm, b = 10 cm
    g) l = 7 cm, b = 1 cm     h) l = 20 cm, b = 11 cm   i) l = 5 cm, b = 4 cm

    m) l = 11 m, b = 9 m      n) l = 18 m, b = 12 m     o) l = 40 m, b = 25 m
    p) l = 100 m, b = 50 m

      i) 2 × (5 + 4) = 2 × 9 = 18 cm
      j) 2 × (14 + 6) = 2 × 20 = 40 cm
      k) 2 × (25 + 15) = 2 × 40 = 80 m
      l) 2 × (30 + 20) = 2 × 50 = 100 m
      m) 2 × (11 + 9) = 2 × 20 = 40 m
      n) 2 × (18 + 12) = 2 × 30 = 60 m
      o) 2 × (40 + 25) = 2 × 65 = 130 m
      p) 2 × (100 + 50) = 2 × 150 = 300 m

M3. Find the perimeter of each triangle.
    a) 5 cm, 7 cm, 9 cm          b) 3 cm, 4 cm, 5 cm
    c) 6 cm, 8 cm, 10 cm         d) 10 cm, 10 cm, 12 cm

             l) 13 + 14 + 15 = 42 cm   m) 3 × 6 = 18 cm   n) 3 × 11 = 33 cm

             m) 10 × 10 = 100 cm   n) 6 × 25 = 150 cm   o) 8 × 15 = 120 cm

M5. Add the sides to find the perimeter of each polygon (all lengths in cm).
    a) 6, 8, 5, 9               b) 12, 7, 12, 9
    c) 4, 4, 6, 6, 5            d) 10, 3, 8, 4, 6

M6. The perimeter of a square is given. Find its side. (side = P ÷ 4.)
    a) P = 36 cm    b) P = 20 cm    c) P = 48 cm    d) P = 100 cm
    e) P = 4 cm     f) P = 60 cm    g) P = 28 cm    h) P = 44 cm

    Answers: a) 36 ÷ 4 = 9 cm   b) 20 ÷ 4 = 5 cm   c) 48 ÷ 4 = 12 cm
             d) 100 ÷ 4 = 25 cm   e) 4 ÷ 4 = 1 cm   f) 60 ÷ 4 = 15 cm
             g) 28 ÷ 4 = 7 cm   h) 44 ÷ 4 = 11 cm   i) 80 ÷ 4 = 20 cm

             m) 96 ÷ 4 = 24 cm   n) 200 ÷ 4 = 50 cm

M7. The perimeter of a rectangle and one side are given. Find the other side.
    (other side = P ÷ 2 − known side.)
    a) P = 30 cm, one side = 9 cm     b) P = 24 cm, one side = 8 cm
    c) P = 50 cm, one side = 15 cm    d) P = 40 cm, one side = 12 cm
    e) P = 36 cm, one side = 10 cm    f) P = 18 cm, one side = 6 cm
    g) P = 100 cm, one side = 30 cm   h) P = 60 cm, one side = 20 cm
    i) P = 26 cm, one side = 7 cm     j) P = 44 cm, one side = 13 cm
    k) P = 80 cm, one side = 25 cm    l) P = 70 cm, one side = 20 cm
    m) P = 22 cm, one side = 10 cm    n) P = 90 cm, one side = 40 cm
    Answers:
      a) (30 ÷ 2) − 9 = 15 − 9 = 6 cm
      b) (24 ÷ 2) − 8 = 12 − 8 = 4 cm
      c) (50 ÷ 2) − 15 = 25 − 15 = 10 cm
      d) (40 ÷ 2) − 12 = 20 − 12 = 8 cm
      e) (36 ÷ 2) − 10 = 18 − 10 = 8 cm
      f) (18 ÷ 2) − 6 = 9 − 6 = 3 cm
      g) (100 ÷ 2) − 30 = 50 − 30 = 20 cm
      h) (60 ÷ 2) − 20 = 30 − 20 = 10 cm
      i) (26 ÷ 2) − 7 = 13 − 7 = 6 cm
      j) (44 ÷ 2) − 13 = 22 − 13 = 9 cm
      k) (80 ÷ 2) − 25 = 40 − 25 = 15 cm
      l) (70 ÷ 2) − 20 = 35 − 20 = 15 cm
      m) (22 ÷ 2) − 10 = 11 − 10 = 1 cm
      n) (90 ÷ 2) − 40 = 45 − 40 = 5 cm

M8. The perimeter and all but one side of a polygon are given. Find the missing
    side.
    a) P = 25 cm; sides 8, 10 cm and __
    b) P = 30 cm; sides 9, 12 cm and __
    c) P = 40 cm; sides 12, 9, 11 cm and __
    d) P = 21 cm; sides 7, 7 cm and __
    e) P = 50 cm; sides 10, 15, 5, 12 cm and __
    f) P = 18 cm; sides 5, 6 cm and __
    g) P = 60 cm; sides 20, 15, 10 cm and __
    h) P = 36 cm; sides 9, 9, 9 cm and __
    i) P = 45 cm; sides 8, 12, 6, 9 cm and __
    j) P = 100 cm; sides 25, 30, 20 cm and __
    k) P = 33 cm; sides 11, 11 cm and __
    l) P = 24 cm; sides 4, 5, 6, 3, 2 cm and __
    m) P = 70 cm; sides 18, 22 cm and __
    n) P = 54 cm; sides 10, 8, 12, 6, 9 cm and __
    Answers:
      a) 25 − (8 + 10) = 25 − 18 = 7 cm
      b) 30 − (9 + 12) = 30 − 21 = 9 cm
      c) 40 − (12 + 9 + 11) = 40 − 32 = 8 cm
      d) 21 − (7 + 7) = 21 − 14 = 7 cm
      e) 50 − (10 + 15 + 5 + 12) = 50 − 42 = 8 cm
      f) 18 − (5 + 6) = 18 − 11 = 7 cm
      g) 60 − (20 + 15 + 10) = 60 − 45 = 15 cm
      h) 36 − (9 + 9 + 9) = 36 − 27 = 9 cm
      i) 45 − (8 + 12 + 6 + 9) = 45 − 35 = 10 cm
      j) 100 − (25 + 30 + 20) = 100 − 75 = 25 cm
      k) 33 − (11 + 11) = 33 − 22 = 11 cm
      l) 24 − (4 + 5 + 6 + 3 + 2) = 24 − 20 = 4 cm
      m) 70 − (18 + 22) = 70 − 40 = 30 cm
      n) 54 − (10 + 8 + 12 + 6 + 9) = 54 − 45 = 9 cm

M9. All corners are right angles. Find the missing sides p and q, then the
    perimeter.
    a)               6 m                  b)               5 m
              +------------+                        +------------+
              |            | 3 m     p              |            | 2 m     p
          8 m |            +--------+           6 m |            +--------+
              |                     |               |                     |
              |                     | q             |                     | q
              |                     |               |                     |
              +---------------------+               +---------------------+
                        10 m                                  9 m
    c)               4 cm                 d)               8 cm
              +------------+                        +------------+

        10 cm |            +--------+          9 cm |            +--------+

         P = 4 + 4 + 8 + 6 + 12 + 10 = 44 cm
      d) p = 15 − 8 = 7 cm, q = 9 − 5 = 4 cm; P = 8 + 5 + 7 + 4 + 15 + 9 = 48 cm
      e) p = 7 − 3 = 4 m, q = 5 − 1 = 4 m; P = 3 + 1 + 4 + 4 + 7 + 5 = 24 m

M10. Each small edge is 1 cm. Count the edges round the outside to find each

M11. Change to centimetres first. Give each perimeter in cm and in m. (1 m =
     100 cm.)
     a) rectangle 2 m by 50 cm        b) rectangle 1 m by 40 cm
     c) rectangle 3 m by 75 cm        d) rectangle 1 m by 1 m
     e) rectangle 5 m by 20 cm        f) rectangle 2 m by 2 m

M12. Compare the perimeters. Write <, > or = between the two shapes, and give
     both perimeters.
     a) square, side 6 cm __ rectangle 8 cm by 3 cm
     b) equilateral triangle, side 8 cm __ square, side 6 cm
     c) regular hexagon, side 5 cm __ regular pentagon, side 6 cm
     d) rectangle 10 cm by 2 cm __ square, side 7 cm
     e) square, side 9 cm __ rectangle 12 cm by 5 cm
     f) rectangle 1 m by 50 cm __ square, side 80 cm
     g) regular octagon, side 4 cm __ square, side 8 cm
     h) triangle 5, 12, 13 cm __ rectangle 9 cm by 6 cm
     i) square, side 15 m __ rectangle 20 m by 9 m
     j) regular decagon, side 3 cm __ equilateral triangle, side 11 cm
     k) rectangle 14 cm by 1 cm __ square, side 7 cm
     l) square, side 1 m __ rectangle 1 m by 99 cm
     Answers:
       a) 24 cm > 22 cm
       b) 24 cm = 24 cm
       c) 30 cm = 30 cm
       d) 24 cm < 28 cm
       e) 36 cm > 34 cm
       f) 300 cm < 320 cm
       g) 32 cm = 32 cm
       h) 30 cm = 30 cm
       i) 60 m > 58 m
       j) 30 cm < 33 cm
       k) 30 cm > 28 cm
       l) 400 cm > 398 cm

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: walk round the edge of a table, a rug or the classroom and count
  steps; lay string round a shape and then measure the string; mark a dot where
  you start and tick each side as you add it; draw shapes on squared paper.
  Harder: find missing sides of shapes with 8 or more sides, find all the
  rectangles with whole-number sides and perimeter 20 cm, work backwards from
  perimeter for regular polygons, and find the perimeter of shapes drawn on
  dotty paper.


================================================================================
TOPIC 4.29 — AREA BY COUNTING SQUARES; AREA OF RECTANGLE = LENGTH × BREADTH
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - AREA is the amount of flat surface inside a shape. We measure it by counting
    how many UNIT SQUARES cover it with no gaps and no overlaps.
  - A square 1 cm long and 1 cm wide has an area of 1 SQUARE CENTIMETRE, written
    1 cm² (or 1 sq cm). A square 1 m by 1 m has an area of 1 m² (used for rooms,
    floors and fields). Length is in cm or m; area is in cm² or m².
  - Counting squares: count the whole squares; two half squares make one whole
    square. For a curved shape, ESTIMATE: count a square if half or more of it
    is inside, leave it out if less than half is inside.
  - A rectangle is ROWS of equal squares, so area = number of rows × squares in
    each row, that is AREA = LENGTH × BREADTH (6 cm by 4 cm gives 6 × 4 = 24
    cm²). A square: AREA = SIDE × SIDE.
  - Know the areas of squares with sides 1 to 12: 1, 4, 9, 16, 25, 36, 49, 64,
    81, 100, 121, 144.
  - Missing side: if you know the area and one side, divide: breadth = area ÷
    length (area 40 cm², length 8 cm, so breadth = 40 ÷ 8 = 5 cm).
  - Area is NOT perimeter: perimeter is the distance round the edge (add the
    sides), area is the space inside (multiply length by breadth).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Meera covers part of her floor with square tiles. Each # is one tile and
    each . is bare floor. How many tiles are there? What is the area of the
    tiled part?
        # # # # .
        # # # # .
        # # # # #
        . . . # #
    Answer: Area = 15 square units (15 tiles).
    Teacher explains: Count row by row and write the number at the end of each
    row: row 1 has 4, row 2 has 4, row 3 has 5, row 4 has 2. Add: 4 + 4 + 5 + 2
    = 15. Tick each square as you count so you never count one twice. The area
    is 15 square units, because 15 unit squares cover it. Now you try: a shape
    with rows of 3, 5, 5 and 2 squares (15 square units).

D2. On a squared page, Arjun draws this shape. # is a whole square and / is half
    a square (a square cut along its diagonal). What is its area?
        # # # /
        # # # #
        / # # /
    Answer: 9 whole squares and 3 halves: 9 + 1 1/2 = 10 1/2 square units.
    Teacher explains: First count the whole squares: 3 + 4 + 2 = 9. Then count
    the half squares: 3. Two halves make one whole, so 3 halves = 1 whole and 1
    half. Total 9 + 1 + 1/2 = 10 1/2 square units. A common mistake is to count
    every half as a whole square (that gives 12, too much). Pair the halves with
    a pencil line: "these two make one". Now you try: 6 whole squares and 4
    halves (8 square units).

D3. Why do we measure area with squares? Why not with circles, like one-rupee
    coins?
    Answer: Squares fit together with no gaps and no overlaps, so they cover a
    shape exactly. Circles always leave gaps between them.
    Teacher explains: Let the child cover a notebook with coins: there are gaps
    between the coins, so the count does not tell us the true space. Then cover
    it with square paper tiles or sticky notes: they fit edge to edge. That is
    why the unit of area is a unit SQUARE. The size of the square must be the
    same every time, which is why we use 1 cm² or 1 m². Now you try: can
    triangles cut from a square tile cover a floor without gaps? (yes, 2 halves
    make each square)

D4. A photo is 6 cm long and 4 cm wide. Mark it into 1 cm squares. How many
    squares are there? Can you find it without counting one by one?
        # # # # # #
        # # # # # #
        # # # # # #
        # # # # # #
    Answer: Area = 24 cm². There are 4 rows of 6 squares: 4 × 6 = 24.
    Teacher explains: Every row has the same number of squares: 6 (the length).
    There are 4 rows (the breadth). So instead of counting 24 squares we
    multiply: 4 rows × 6 = 24. This is why AREA OF A RECTANGLE = LENGTH ×
    BREADTH. Write the unit: cm² (square centimetres), not cm. Draw a 5 cm by 3
    cm rectangle on squared paper and check by counting that 5 × 3 = 15. Now you
    try: a card 7 cm long and 5 cm wide (35 cm²).

D5. A classroom floor is 8 m long and 6 m wide. What is its area?
    Answer: Area = 8 × 6 = 48 m².
    Teacher explains: Length × breadth = 8 m × 6 m = 48 m². Picture the floor
    marked into squares of 1 m by 1 m: 6 rows of 8 squares. Metres times metres
    gives SQUARE metres, m². For a big floor we use m², for a page we use cm².
    Ask: "Would the area of a floor in cm² be a big or a small number?" (very
    big: 1 m² = 100 × 100 = 10,000 cm²). Now you try: a hall 12 m long and 9 m
    wide (108 m²).

D6. A square handkerchief has sides of 9 cm. What is its area?
    Answer: Area = 9 × 9 = 81 cm².
    Teacher explains: A square is a rectangle whose length and breadth are
    equal, so area = side × side = 9 × 9 = 81 cm². A common mistake is 9 × 4 =
    36 (that is the perimeter, in cm). Learn the square areas 1, 4, 9, 16, 25,
    36, 49, 64, 81, 100, 121, 144 for sides 1 to 12 — they are the square
    numbers. Now you try: a square tile of side 12 cm (144 cm²).

D7. A vegetable garden has an area of 60 m². It is 12 m long. How wide is it?
    Answer: Breadth = 60 ÷ 12 = 5 m.
    Teacher explains: Area = length × breadth, so 12 × __ = 60. Ask: "12 times
    what is 60?" 12 × 5 = 60, so the breadth is 5 m. This is the same as 60 ÷ 12
    = 5. Check: 12 × 5 = 60. Draw a bar: 60 squares in rows of 12 make 5 rows.
    Watch the unit: the answer is a LENGTH, so it is 5 m, not 5 m². Now you try:
    a rug of area 56 m² that is 8 m long (7 m wide).

D8. Ravi says the area of a rectangle 5 cm by 3 cm is 16 cm². What did he do
    wrong?
    Answer: He found the perimeter: 5 + 3 + 5 + 3 = 16 cm. The area is 5 × 3 =
    15 cm².
    Teacher explains: Draw the rectangle and mark it into squares: 3 rows of 5 =
    15 squares. Then walk a finger round the edge: 5 + 3 + 5 + 3 = 16 cm.
    Perimeter is the FENCE around the field (cm); area is the GRASS inside
    (cm²). Say "fence: add, grass: multiply". The numbers 15 and 16 are close
    here only by chance. Now you try: the area and the perimeter of 6 cm by 2 cm
    (12 cm² and 16 cm).

D9. Priya traces a leaf on 1 cm squared paper. # means a square fully inside the
    leaf, o means a square that is half or more inside, . means less than half
    inside. Estimate the area of the leaf.
        . o o .
        o # # o
        # # # #
        o # # o
        . o . .
    Answer: About 8 + 7 = 15 cm².
    Teacher explains: For a curved shape we cannot get the exact area by
    counting, so we ESTIMATE. Count the full squares: 8. Count the squares that
    are half or more inside: 7 — count each as 1. Leave out the squares that are
    less than half inside (they roughly make up for the bits we added).
    Estimate: 8 + 7 = 15 cm². Say "about 15 cm²". Now you try: a shape with 12
    full squares and 6 squares more than half inside (about 18 cm²).

D10. A verandah is 4 m long and 3 m wide. It is covered with square tiles of 1 m
     by 1 m. Each tile costs ₹250. How many tiles are needed and what is the
     cost?
     Answer: Area = 4 × 3 = 12 m², so 12 tiles. Cost = 12 × ₹250 = ₹3,000.
     Teacher explains: Each tile covers 1 m², so the number of tiles is the area
     in m². Step 1: area = 4 × 3 = 12 m², 12 tiles. Step 2: cost = 12 × 250.
     Work it as 10 × 250 = 2,500 and 2 × 250 = 500, total ₹3,000. Draw the
     verandah as 3 rows of 4 tiles to check the 12. Now you try: a floor 5 m by
     4 m with ₹300 tiles (20 tiles, ₹6,000).

D11. Which has the bigger area: a rectangle 7 cm by 5 cm or a square of side 6
     cm? By how much?
     Answer: The square: 6 × 6 = 36 cm², the rectangle 7 × 5 = 35 cm². The
     square is bigger by 1 cm².
     Teacher explains: Find both areas, then compare. Rectangle: 7 × 5 = 35 cm².
     Square: 6 × 6 = 36 cm². 36 − 35 = 1 cm². Both shapes have the same
     perimeter (24 cm), but not the same area — we meet this idea again in topic
     4.30. You cannot tell the bigger area by looking at the longest side. Now
     you try: rectangle 9 cm by 4 cm or square of side 6 cm? (the same: 36 cm²)

D12. A rug is 4 m by 3 m. What happens to its area if the length is doubled?
     What if both the length and the breadth are doubled?
     Answer: Length doubled: 8 × 3 = 24 m² (twice 12). Both doubled: 8 × 6 = 48
     m² (4 times 12).
     Teacher explains: Draw it on squared paper. The first rug is 3 rows of 4 =
     12 squares. Doubling the length puts a second copy beside it: 2 copies, 24
     m². Doubling both makes 2 copies across and 2 copies down: 4 copies, 48 m².
     Many children think doubling both sides doubles the area — the picture
     shows it is 4 times. Now you try: a 2 cm by 5 cm card with both sides
     tripled (6 × 15 = 90 cm², 9 times 10).

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Each # is one unit square. Find the area (in square units).
    a)       b)     c)       d)         e)       f)
    # # #    # #    # . .    # # # #    . # .    # # # # #
    # # #    # #    # . .    . # # .    # # #    # . . . #
             # #    # # #               . # .
             # #

    g)       h)         i)         j)           k)           l)
    # # .    # # # #    # . # .    # # # # #    # # # # #    . . # . .
    . # #    # # # #    # # # #    # # # # #    # . . . #    . # # # .
    . . #    # # # #    # . # .    . . # . .    # # # # #    # # # # #
    Answers: a) 6   b) 8   c) 5   d) 6   e) 5   f) 7   g) 5   h) 12   i) 8
             j) 11   k) 12   l) 9

M2. # is a whole square and / is half a square. Find the area in square units.
    a)     b)       c)       d)     e)       f)
    # /    / # /    # # /    / /    # # #    . /
    # /             # # /    / /    / # /    / #
                                             # #

    g)         h)       i)       j)         k)           l)
    / # # /    / # /    / . /    # # # /    / # # # /    / # # /
    # # # #    # # #    # # #    # # /                   # # # #
               / # /             # /                     / # # /
    Answers: a) 3   b) 2   c) 5   d) 2   e) 5   f) 4   g) 7   h) 7   i) 4
             j) 7 1/2   k) 4   l) 10

M3. Find the area of each rectangle (length × breadth).
    a) 3 cm × 2 cm     b) 4 cm × 5 cm     c) 6 cm × 3 cm     d) 7 cm × 4 cm
    e) 8 cm × 5 cm     f) 9 cm × 6 cm     g) 6 cm × 7 cm     h) 8 cm × 8 cm
    i) 9 cm × 7 cm     j) 10 cm × 4 cm    k) 12 cm × 3 cm    l) 11 cm × 6 cm
    m) 12 cm × 9 cm    n) 7 cm × 7 cm     o) 5 cm × 9 cm     p) 8 cm × 12 cm
    q) 1 cm × 9 cm     r) 15 cm × 4 cm    s) 20 cm × 6 cm    t) 25 cm × 3 cm
    u) 12 cm × 12 cm   v) 13 cm × 2 cm    w) 30 cm × 5 cm    x) 10 cm × 10 cm
    Answers: a) 6 cm²   b) 20 cm²   c) 18 cm²   d) 28 cm²   e) 40 cm²
             f) 54 cm²   g) 42 cm²   h) 64 cm²   i) 63 cm²   j) 40 cm²
             k) 36 cm²   l) 66 cm²   m) 108 cm²   n) 49 cm²   o) 45 cm²
             p) 96 cm²   q) 9 cm²   r) 60 cm²   s) 120 cm²   t) 75 cm²
             u) 144 cm²   v) 26 cm²   w) 150 cm²   x) 100 cm²

M4. Find the area of the square with the given side.
    a) side 1 cm    b) side 2 cm    c) side 3 cm    d) side 4 cm
    e) side 5 cm    f) side 6 cm    g) side 7 cm    h) side 8 cm
    i) side 9 cm    j) side 10 cm   k) side 11 cm   l) side 12 cm
    m) side 15 cm   n) side 20 cm   o) side 25 cm   p) side 30 cm
    Answers: a) 1 cm²   b) 4 cm²   c) 9 cm²   d) 16 cm²   e) 25 cm²   f) 36 cm²
             g) 49 cm²   h) 64 cm²   i) 81 cm²   j) 100 cm²   k) 121 cm²
             l) 144 cm²   m) 225 cm²   n) 400 cm²   o) 625 cm²   p) 900 cm²

M5. Find the area in m².
    a) 12 m × 8 m      b) 25 m × 4 m      c) 30 m × 20 m     d) 15 m × 15 m
    e) 100 m × 3 m     f) 40 m × 5 m      g) 18 m × 10 m     h) 50 m × 50 m
    i) 24 m × 6 m      j) 35 m × 2 m      k) 16 m × 9 m      l) 60 m × 7 m
    m) 45 m × 20 m     n) 100 m × 100 m   o) 27 m × 3 m      p) 200 m × 4 m
    Answers: a) 96 m²   b) 100 m²   c) 600 m²   d) 225 m²   e) 300 m²
             f) 200 m²   g) 180 m²   h) 2,500 m²   i) 144 m²   j) 70 m²
             k) 144 m²   l) 420 m²   m) 900 m²   n) 10,000 m²   o) 81 m²
             p) 800 m²

M6. The area and one side of a rectangle are given. Find the other side.
    a) A = 24 cm², side 6 cm     b) A = 35 cm², side 5 cm
    c) A = 42 cm², side 7 cm     d) A = 56 cm², side 8 cm
    e) A = 72 cm², side 9 cm     f) A = 30 cm², side 3 cm
    g) A = 48 cm², side 4 cm     h) A = 63 cm², side 9 cm
    i) A = 80 cm², side 10 cm    j) A = 96 cm², side 12 cm
    k) A = 54 cm², side 6 cm     l) A = 132 cm², side 11 cm
    m) A = 100 cm², side 25 cm   n) A = 60 cm², side 15 cm
    o) A = 45 cm², side 45 cm    p) A = 84 cm², side 7 cm
    q) A = 120 cm², side 8 cm    r) A = 7 cm², side 7 cm
    s) A = 1 cm², side 1 cm      t) A = 200 cm², side 40 cm
    Answers: a) 4 cm   b) 7 cm   c) 6 cm   d) 7 cm   e) 8 cm   f) 10 cm
             g) 12 cm   h) 7 cm   i) 8 cm   j) 8 cm   k) 9 cm   l) 12 cm
             m) 4 cm   n) 4 cm   o) 1 cm   p) 12 cm   q) 15 cm   r) 1 cm
             s) 1 cm   t) 5 cm

M7. A square has this area. How long is each side?
    a) 49 cm²    b) 16 cm²    c) 81 cm²    d) 1 cm²     e) 144 cm²   f) 36 cm²
    g) 100 cm²   h) 9 cm²     i) 64 cm²    j) 121 cm²   k) 25 cm²    l) 4 cm²

    Answers: a) 7 cm   b) 4 cm   c) 9 cm   d) 1 cm   e) 12 cm   f) 6 cm
             g) 10 cm   h) 3 cm   i) 8 cm   j) 11 cm   k) 5 cm   l) 2 cm

M8. Fill in the blanks for each rectangle.
         length   breadth   area
    a)  7 cm     3 cm      __
    b)  9 cm     __        54 cm²
    c)  __       8 cm      96 cm²
    d)  12 cm    11 cm     __

    f)  15 cm    __        75 cm²
    g)  4 cm     __        4 cm²
    h)  __       10 cm     250 cm²
    i)  20 cm    20 cm     __
    j)  __       9 cm      108 cm²
    k)  8 cm     __        40 cm²
    l)  11 cm    __        99 cm²
    Answers: a) 21 cm²   b) 6 cm   c) 12 cm   d) 132 cm²   e) 7 cm   f) 5 cm
             g) 1 cm   h) 25 cm   i) 400 cm²   j) 12 cm   k) 5 cm   l) 9 cm

M9. Which rectangle has the larger area, and by how many cm²? (All lengths in
    cm.)
    a) 6 × 4  or  5 × 5     b) 8 × 3  or  5 × 5     c) 9 × 2  or  4 × 4
    d) 10 × 3  or  6 × 5    e) 12 × 2  or  5 × 5    f) 7 × 6  or  8 × 5
    g) 9 × 4  or  6 × 6     h) 11 × 3  or  7 × 5    i) 12 × 6  or  9 × 8
    j) 20 × 2  or  6 × 7    k) 8 × 8  or  10 × 6    l) 15 × 4  or  12 × 5
    Answers:
      a) second, 24 < 25, by 1
      b) second, 24 < 25, by 1
      c) first, 18 > 16, by 2
      d) same, 30 = 30
      e) second, 24 < 25, by 1
      f) first, 42 > 40, by 2
      g) same, 36 = 36
      h) second, 33 < 35, by 2
      i) same, 72 = 72
      j) second, 40 < 42, by 2
      k) first, 64 > 60, by 4
      l) same, 60 = 60

M10. True or false? Correct the false ones.
     a) 4 cm × 5 cm rectangle: area = 20 cm²
     b) square of side 7 cm: area = 14 cm²
     c) square of side 10 cm: area = 100 cm²
     d) 3 cm × 8 cm rectangle: area = 22 cm²
     e) area of a room can be written in m²
     f) area of 6 cm × 6 cm = 6 cm × 4
     g) 2 half squares = 1 whole square
     h) 5 whole + 4 half squares = 9 square units
     i) 9 cm × 4 cm and 6 cm × 6 cm have equal areas
     j) area 30 cm², length 6 cm → breadth 5 cm
     k) area 48 m², breadth 4 m → length 44 m
     l) 1 m² = 100 cm²
     m) doubling both sides doubles the area
     n) a 1 cm × 12 cm strip has area 12 cm²
     Answers:
       a) true
       b) false (49 cm²)
       c) true
       d) false (24 cm²; 22 cm is the perimeter)
       e) true
       f) false (36 cm²; 24 cm is the perimeter)
       g) true
       h) false (7 square units)
       i) true
       j) true
       k) false (12 m)
       l) false (1 m² = 100 × 100 = 10,000 cm²)
       m) false (it becomes 4 times)
       n) true

M11. Find the area (bigger numbers; use your multiplication methods).
     a) 23 m × 4 m    b) 35 m × 6 m    c) 48 m × 3 m    d) 19 m × 5 m
     e) 64 m × 7 m    f) 125 m × 4 m   g) 37 m × 9 m    h) 56 m × 8 m
     i) 72 m × 5 m    j) 99 m × 3 m    k) 84 m × 6 m    l) 150 m × 6 m
     m) 208 m × 5 m   n) 45 m × 9 m    o) 13 m × 13 m   p) 25 m × 25 m
     Answers: a) 92 m²   b) 210 m²   c) 144 m²   d) 95 m²   e) 448 m²
              f) 500 m²   g) 333 m²   h) 448 m²   i) 360 m²   j) 297 m²
              k) 504 m²   l) 900 m²   m) 1,040 m²   n) 405 m²   o) 169 m²
              p) 625 m²

M12. Find the area in square units. For the last four, find how many half
     squares there are.
     a) 10 whole + 4 halves
     b) 6 whole + 2 halves
     c) 12 whole + 6 halves
     d) 3 whole + 1 half
     e) 8 whole + 3 halves
     f) 0 whole + 8 halves
     g) 15 whole + 10 halves
     h) 7 whole + 5 halves
     i) 20 whole + 1 half
     j) 9 whole + 9 halves
     k) 4 whole + 12 halves
     l) 1 whole + 1 half
     m) area 7 1/2, 5 wholes: halves = __
     n) area 10 1/2, 6 wholes: halves = __
     o) area 4 1/2, 3 wholes: halves = __
     p) area 2 1/2, 0 wholes: halves = __
     Answers: a) 12   b) 7   c) 15   d) 3 1/2   e) 9 1/2   f) 4   g) 20

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: let the child build rectangles with square tiles or cut paper
  squares, count them one by one, then count the rows and the squares in a row,
  and only then write length × breadth. Always say the unit aloud ("square
  centimetres"). Keep separate the words "round the edge" (perimeter) and
  "inside" (area). Harder: measure real things (a table top, a notebook, the
  classroom) and find their areas; find how many 1 m² tiles a room needs and the
  cost; find all the rectangles with area 36 cm² (topic 4.30); estimate the area
  of a hand traced on squared paper.


================================================================================
TOPIC 4.30 — AREA VS PERIMETER: SAME AREA DIFFERENT PERIMETER AND VICE VERSA
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - PERIMETER is the distance round the edge (in cm or m). AREA is the space
    inside (in cm² or m²). For a rectangle: perimeter = 2 × (length + breadth),
    area = length × breadth.
  - SAME AREA, DIFFERENT PERIMETER: 24 squares can make rectangles 1 × 24
    (perimeter 50), 2 × 12 (28), 3 × 8 (22) and 4 × 6 (20). The long thin one
    has the biggest perimeter; the one closest to a square has the smallest.
  - SAME PERIMETER, DIFFERENT AREA: with perimeter 20 we can make 1 × 9 (area
    9), 2 × 8 (16), 3 × 7 (21), 4 × 6 (24) and 5 × 5 (25). The square has the
    biggest area; the long thin one the smallest.
  - To find ALL the rectangles with a given area, use the factor pairs of the
    area (topic 4.13). To find all the rectangles with a given perimeter, halve
    the perimeter: length + breadth = half the perimeter.
  - A rectangle with whole-number sides always has an EVEN perimeter.
  - A bigger area does not always mean a bigger perimeter, and a bigger
    perimeter does not always mean a bigger area. Always work both out.
  - Moving or adding squares in a shape can change the perimeter even when the
    area stays the same.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. A farmer wants a rectangular pen for his goats with an area of 24 m². The
    sides must be whole metres. Which rectangles can he make? Which needs the
    least fencing?
    Answer: All the rectangles have area 24 m²:
        length  breadth  area   perimeter
        24 m    1 m      24 m²  50 m
        12 m    2 m      24 m²  28 m
        8 m     3 m      24 m²  22 m
        6 m     4 m      24 m²  20 m
    The 6 m by 4 m pen needs the least fencing: 20 m.
    Teacher explains: List the factor pairs of 24 in order, so none is missed: 1
    × 24, 2 × 12, 3 × 8, 4 × 6. The next would be 6 × 4, which is the same pen
    turned round, so stop. For each pen, perimeter = 2 × (length + breadth): 2 ×
    (24 + 1) = 50, 2 × (12 + 2) = 28, 2 × (8 + 3) = 22, 2 × (6 + 4) = 20. Same
    area, very different fences! The fatter the rectangle, the less fence. Now
    you try: area 18 m² (1 × 18: 38 m, 2 × 9: 22 m, 3 × 6: 18 m; least 3 × 6).

D2. Ananya has 20 m of rope to mark a rectangle for a game of kabaddi practice.
    The sides must be whole metres. Which rectangle gives the biggest playing
    area?
    Answer: Length + breadth = 20 ÷ 2 = 10 m.
        length  breadth  perimeter  area
        9 m     1 m      20 m       9 m²
        8 m     2 m      20 m       16 m²
        7 m     3 m      20 m       21 m²
        6 m     4 m      20 m       24 m²
        5 m     5 m      20 m       25 m²
    The 5 m by 5 m square gives the biggest area: 25 m².
    Teacher explains: The rope goes all round, so the perimeter is 20 m. Two
    sides (one length and one breadth) use half of it: 10 m. So look for pairs
    that add to 10: 9 + 1, 8 + 2, 7 + 3, 6 + 4, 5 + 5. Multiply each pair for
    the area: 9, 16, 21, 24, 25. Same perimeter, different areas, and the square
    wins. A common mistake is to use pairs adding to 20 — that would need 40 m
    of rope. Now you try: perimeter 16 m (7 × 1 = 7, 6 × 2 = 12, 5 × 3 = 15, 4 ×
    4 = 16; biggest 4 × 4).

D3. Four square tiles of side 1 m are placed in a row, and then as a 2 × 2
    square. Do the two shapes have the same area? The same perimeter?
        row:       square:
        # # # #    # #
                   # #
    Answer: Same area: 4 m² each. Different perimeters: the row has 4 + 1 + 4 +
    1 = 10 m, the square has 2 + 2 + 2 + 2 = 8 m.
    Teacher explains: The tiles are the same, so the area (space covered) does
    not change: 4 m². But the edge changes. In the row, the tiles touch along
    only 3 edges; in the square, they touch along 4 edges, and every edge that
    is shared is hidden inside, not part of the perimeter. More hidden edges
    means a smaller perimeter. Let the child move real tiles and count the
    outside edges. Now you try: 6 tiles as a 1 × 6 row and as a 2 × 3 rectangle
    (both 6 m²; perimeters 14 m and 10 m).

D4. Tanvi says, "If a rectangle has a bigger area, it must also have a bigger
    perimeter." Is she right?
    Answer: No. A 10 cm by 1 cm strip has area 10 cm² and perimeter 22 cm. A 4
    cm by 4 cm square has a bigger area (16 cm²) but a smaller perimeter (16
    cm).
    Teacher explains: To show a statement is wrong, one example that breaks it
    is enough (a counter-example). Work out both measures for each shape and put
    them in a table: strip 10 cm², 22 cm; square 16 cm², 16 cm. The square wins
    on area, the strip wins on perimeter. So area and perimeter are different
    measures and must each be worked out. Now you try: compare 12 × 1 and 5 × 5
    (areas 12 and 25, perimeters 26 and 20).

D5. A school garden must have an area of 36 m² and whole-metre sides. Fencing
    costs ₹100 a metre. Which shape is cheapest to fence, and which is dearest?
    Answer: Cheapest: 6 m by 6 m, perimeter 24 m, ₹2,400. Dearest: 36 m by 1 m,
    perimeter 74 m, ₹7,400.
    Teacher explains: Factor pairs of 36: 1 × 36, 2 × 18, 3 × 12, 4 × 9, 6 × 6.
    Perimeters: 74, 40, 30, 26, 24 m. The square uses the least fence. Cost =
    perimeter × ₹100: 24 × 100 = ₹2,400 and 74 × 100 = ₹7,400. Point out the
    pattern: as the sides get closer together, the perimeter gets smaller. Now
    you try: area 16 m² at ₹50 a metre (4 × 4: 16 m, ₹800; 1 × 16: 34 m,
    ₹1,700).

D6. Rahul bends a 16 cm piece of wire into a rectangle with whole centimetre
    sides. List all the rectangles. Which one has the biggest area?
    Answer: 7 × 1 (7 cm²), 6 × 2 (12 cm²), 5 × 3 (15 cm²), 4 × 4 (16 cm²). The 4
    cm square has the biggest area.
    Teacher explains: Half of 16 is 8, so length + breadth = 8. Go down in
    order: 7 + 1, 6 + 2, 5 + 3, 4 + 4. Multiply for the areas: 7, 12, 15, 16.
    The wire (perimeter) is always 16 cm, but the space inside grows as the
    shape gets more square. Let the child bend a piece of string round pins on a
    board to see it. Now you try: a 12 cm wire (5 × 1 = 5, 4 × 2 = 8, 3 × 3 = 9
    cm²; the square).

D7. Sita has 12 square tiles. She wants to arrange all of them into one
    rectangle. How many different rectangles can she make? Which has the
    shortest border?
    Answer: Three: 1 × 12 (perimeter 26), 2 × 6 (perimeter 16) and 3 × 4
    (perimeter 14). The 3 × 4 has the shortest border.
    Teacher explains: 12 tiles: rows × tiles in a row = 12, so use the factor
    pairs 1 and 12, 2 and 6, 3 and 4. (4 × 3 is the 3 × 4 turned.) Every
    rectangle has area 12 square units. Perimeters: 2 × 13 = 26, 2 × 8 = 16, 2 ×
    7 = 14. With 7 tiles, only one rectangle is possible (1 × 7), because 7 is
    prime. Now you try: 20 tiles (1 × 20: 42, 2 × 10: 24, 4 × 5: 18).

D8. An L-shape is made of 3 squares of side 1 cm. One more square is added to
    fill the corner. What happens to the area and to the perimeter?
        before:    after:
        # .        # #
        # #        # #
    Answer: Area goes up from 3 cm² to 4 cm², but the perimeter stays 8 cm.
    Teacher explains: Count the outside edges one by one, going round: the L has
    8 edges of 1 cm, and the 2 × 2 square also has 8. The new square adds 2 new
    outside edges but hides 2 old ones, so the perimeter is unchanged. If
    instead the new square is stuck on the end of a row (touching one edge), it
    adds 3 edges and hides 1: the perimeter grows by 2. So adding area can leave
    the perimeter the same or make it bigger. Now you try: add a square to the
    end of a row of 3 (area 3 → 4, perimeter 8 → 10 cm).

D9. Two carpets both have a perimeter of 18 m. One is 7 m by 2 m; the other is 5
    m by 4 m. Which carpet covers more floor?
    Answer: The 5 m by 4 m carpet: 20 m² against 14 m² — 6 m² more.
    Teacher explains: Check the perimeters first: 2 × (7 + 2) = 18 and 2 × (5 +
    4) = 18. Same border! Now the areas: 7 × 2 = 14 m² and 5 × 4 = 20 m². The
    same length of border can hold different amounts of floor. Ask: "Can you
    find a carpet with perimeter 18 m that covers even more?" (no whole-metre
    one: 4 + 5 is the closest pair to equal). Now you try: perimeter 22 m: 9 m
    by 2 m or 6 m by 5 m? (6 × 5 = 30 m², more than 18 m²)

D10. Can a rectangle with whole-number sides have a perimeter of 15 cm? Explain
     why.
     Answer: No. Perimeter = 2 × (length + breadth), which is always an even
     number. 15 is odd.
     Teacher explains: Every length appears twice (top and bottom) and every
     breadth twice (left and right). A number used twice gives a double, and all
     doubles are even. So perimeters like 10, 12, 14 cm are possible, but 15 cm
     is not (with whole-number sides). With half-centimetres, like 4 1/2 cm by 3
     cm, it can happen. Now you try: can the perimeter be 30 cm? (yes, e.g. 10 ×
     5; half is 15)

D11. A square of side 4 cm has area 16 cm² and perimeter 16 cm. Are the area and
     the perimeter equal?
     Answer: No. The NUMBERS are equal, but 16 cm² is a space and 16 cm is a
     length — they cannot be compared. Only the numbers happen to match.
     Teacher explains: Change the unit to show it: in millimetres the square is
     40 mm by 40 mm, area 1,600 mm² and perimeter 160 mm — no longer the same
     number! There is one more whole-number rectangle whose numbers match: 6 by
     3 (area 18, perimeter 18). Let the child test other rectangles and see that
     the numbers usually differ. Now you try: a 5 × 5 square: area and perimeter
     numbers? (25 and 20)

D12. For a rangoli competition, Kavya can decorate a square card of side 5 cm or
     a rectangular card 8 cm by 3 cm. She puts ribbon round the edge and paint
     inside. Which card needs more ribbon? Which needs more paint?
     Answer: Ribbon (perimeter): square 20 cm, rectangle 22 cm — the rectangle
     needs more. Paint (area): square 25 cm², rectangle 24 cm² — the square
     needs more.
     Teacher explains: Ribbon goes round the edge, so find perimeters: 4 × 5 =
     20 cm and 2 × (8 + 3) = 22 cm. Paint covers the inside, so find areas: 5 ×
     5 = 25 cm² and 8 × 3 = 24 cm². The shape with more paint has less ribbon!
     Always ask first: "Is this about the edge or about the inside?" Now you
     try: square of side 6 cm or rectangle 9 cm by 4 cm (ribbon 24 and 26 cm;
     paint 36 and 36 cm²).

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Find the area (cm²) and the perimeter (cm) of each rectangle.
    a) 5 × 3     b) 6 × 4     c) 8 × 2     d) 7 × 7     e) 9 × 1     f) 10 × 5
    g) 12 × 3    h) 6 × 6     i) 11 × 2    j) 8 × 5     k) 15 × 1    l) 9 × 4
    m) 12 × 12   n) 20 × 3    o) 10 × 10   p) 16 × 1    q) 7 × 3     r) 4 × 4
    s) 25 × 2    t) 13 × 6
    Answers: a) A 15, P 16   b) A 24, P 20   c) A 16, P 20   d) A 49, P 28
             e) A 9, P 20   f) A 50, P 30   g) A 36, P 30   h) A 36, P 24
             i) A 22, P 26   j) A 40, P 26   k) A 15, P 32   l) A 36, P 26
             m) A 144, P 48   n) A 60, P 46   o) A 100, P 40   p) A 16, P 34
             q) A 21, P 20   r) A 16, P 16   s) A 50, P 54   t) A 78, P 38

M2. List all the rectangles with whole-number sides that have this area. Give
    the perimeter (P) of each.
    a) area 12   b) area 16   c) area 18   d) area 20   e) area 30   f) area 36
    g) area 7    h) area 13   i) area 1    j) area 9    k) area 25   l) area 32
    Answers:
      a) 1 × 12: P 26, 2 × 6: P 16, 3 × 4: P 14
      b) 1 × 16: P 34, 2 × 8: P 20, 4 × 4: P 16
      c) 1 × 18: P 38, 2 × 9: P 22, 3 × 6: P 18
      d) 1 × 20: P 42, 2 × 10: P 24, 4 × 5: P 18
      e) 1 × 30: P 62, 2 × 15: P 34, 3 × 10: P 26, 5 × 6: P 22
      f) 1 × 36: P 74, 2 × 18: P 40, 3 × 12: P 30, 4 × 9: P 26, 6 × 6: P 24
      g) 1 × 7: P 16
      h) 1 × 13: P 28
      i) 1 × 1: P 4
      j) 1 × 9: P 20, 3 × 3: P 12
      k) 1 × 25: P 52, 5 × 5: P 20
      l) 1 × 32: P 66, 2 × 16: P 36, 4 × 8: P 24

M3. Look at all the whole-number rectangles with this area. Which has the
    smallest perimeter and which the largest?
    a) area 8     b) area 10    c) area 28    d) area 40    e) area 48
    f) area 60    g) area 64    h) area 72    i) area 100   j) area 45
    k) area 15    l) area 81

      a) smallest P 12 (2 × 4), largest P 18 (1 × 8)

      e) smallest P 28 (6 × 8), largest P 98 (1 × 48)
      f) smallest P 32 (6 × 10), largest P 122 (1 × 60)
      g) smallest P 32 (8 × 8), largest P 130 (1 × 64)
      h) smallest P 34 (8 × 9), largest P 146 (1 × 72)

      k) smallest P 16 (3 × 5), largest P 32 (1 × 15)
      l) smallest P 36 (9 × 9), largest P 164 (1 × 81)

M4. List all the rectangles with whole-number sides that have this perimeter.
    Give the area (A) of each. Which is largest?
    a) P = 8    b) P = 10   c) P = 12   d) P = 14   e) P = 16   f) P = 18
    Answers:

      d) 1 × 6: A 6, 2 × 5: A 10, 3 × 4: A 12; largest 3 × 4
      e) 1 × 7: A 7, 2 × 6: A 12, 3 × 5: A 15, 4 × 4: A 16; largest 4 × 4
      f) 1 × 8: A 8, 2 × 7: A 14, 3 × 6: A 18, 4 × 5: A 20; largest 4 × 5

M5. The same for these perimeters. Which rectangle has the largest area?
    a) P = 20   b) P = 22   c) P = 24   d) P = 26   e) P = 28   f) P = 30
    Answers:
      a) 1 × 9: A 9, 2 × 8: A 16, 3 × 7: A 21, 4 × 6: A 24, 5 × 5: A 25;
         largest 5 × 5
      b) 1 × 10: A 10, 2 × 9: A 18, 3 × 8: A 24, 4 × 7: A 28, 5 × 6: A 30;
         largest 5 × 6
      c) 1 × 11: A 11, 2 × 10: A 20, 3 × 9: A 27, 4 × 8: A 32, 5 × 7: A 35,
         6 × 6: A 36; largest 6 × 6
      d) 1 × 12: A 12, 2 × 11: A 22, 3 × 10: A 30, 4 × 9: A 36, 5 × 8: A 40,
         6 × 7: A 42; largest 6 × 7
      e) 1 × 13: A 13, 2 × 12: A 24, 3 × 11: A 33, 4 × 10: A 40, 5 × 9: A 45,
         6 × 8: A 48, 7 × 7: A 49; largest 7 × 7
      f) 1 × 14: A 14, 2 × 13: A 26, 3 × 12: A 36, 4 × 11: A 44, 5 × 10: A 50,
         6 × 9: A 54, 7 × 8: A 56; largest 7 × 8

M6. The perimeter P and one side are given. Find the other side and the area A.
    a) P 20, side 6     b) P 24, side 5     c) P 30, side 10
    d) P 16, side 7     e) P 40, side 15    f) P 18, side 4
    g) P 50, side 20    h) P 12, side 3     i) P 36, side 9
    j) P 28, side 13    k) P 44, side 11    l) P 60, side 25
    m) P 14, side 2     n) P 100, side 30   o) P 22, side 10
    p) P 32, side 8

             f) 5, A 20   g) 5, A 100   h) 3, A 9   i) 9, A 81   j) 1, A 13
             k) 11, A 121   l) 5, A 125   m) 5, A 10   n) 20, A 600   o) 1, A 10
             p) 8, A 64

M7. The area A and one side are given. Find the other side and the perimeter P.
    a) A 36, side 4     b) A 40, side 8     c) A 24, side 3
    d) A 50, side 5     e) A 64, side 16    f) A 45, side 9
    g) A 72, side 6     h) A 48, side 12    i) A 30, side 2
    j) A 100, side 10   k) A 56, side 7     l) A 90, side 15
    m) A 81, side 9     n) A 60, side 20    o) A 35, side 1
    p) A 120, side 12

             f) 5, P 28   g) 12, P 36   h) 4, P 32   i) 15, P 34   j) 10, P 40
             k) 8, P 30   l) 6, P 42   m) 9, P 36   n) 3, P 46   o) 35, P 72
             p) 10, P 44

M8. Which has the larger area? Which has the larger perimeter? (Write "same" if
    equal.)
    a) 6 × 2  and  4 × 3    b) 10 × 1  and  4 × 4   c) 8 × 3  and  6 × 4
    d) 12 × 2  and  5 × 5   e) 9 × 4  and  6 × 6    f) 7 × 5  and  9 × 3

      h) A 64, 64: same; P 32, 40: second
      i) A 20, 20: same; P 18, 24: second

      k) A 11, 18: second; P 24, 18: first
      l) A 81, 72: first; P 36, 36: same

M9. Fill in the table (lengths in cm, areas in cm²).
         length  breadth  perimeter  area
    a)  6       4        __         __

    c)  8       __       __         40
    d)  5       __       22         __

M10. Each # is a square of side 1 cm. Find the area A (cm²) and the perimeter P
     (cm) of each shape.
     a)           b)       c)         d)       e)       f)
     # # # # #    # # #    # # # #    # # #    # # .    # # #
                  # # #    # . . .    . # .    . # #    # . #
                                                        # # #

     g)       h)         i)         j)             k)           l)
     . # .    # # # #    # . . .    # # # # # #    # . # . #    # # # #
     # # #    # # # #    # # . .    # # # # # #    # # # # #    # . . #
     . # .    # # # #    # # # .                                # . . #
                         # # # #                                # # # #
     Answers: a) A 5, P 12   b) A 6, P 10   c) A 5, P 12   d) A 4, P 10
              e) A 4, P 10   f) A 8, P 16   g) A 5, P 12   h) A 12, P 14
              i) A 10, P 16   j) A 12, P 16   k) A 8, P 18   l) A 12, P 24

M11. True or false?
     a) Rectangles with the same area always have the same perimeter.
     b) Rectangles with the same perimeter can have different areas.
     c) 3 × 8 and 4 × 6 have the same area.
     d) 3 × 8 and 4 × 6 have the same perimeter.
     e) 2 × 7 and 4 × 5 have the same perimeter.
     f) For area 36, the 6 × 6 square has the smallest perimeter.
     g) For perimeter 24, the 1 × 11 rectangle has the largest area.
     h) A whole-number rectangle can have perimeter 21.
     i) A bigger perimeter always means a bigger area.
     j) A 4 × 4 square: area and perimeter are the same number.
     k) A 6 × 3 rectangle: area and perimeter are the same number.
     l) Area 7 can be made only as 1 × 7 (whole numbers).
     m) Perimeter 10 can be made as 4 × 1 and 3 × 2.
     n) Filling the corner of an L of 3 squares changes the perimeter.
     Answers:
       a) false
       b) true
       c) true
       d) false (22 and 20)
       e) true (18)
       f) true
       g) false (6 × 6, area 36)
       h) false (always even)
       i) false (e.g. 9 × 1: P 20, A 9; 4 × 4: P 16, A 16)
       j) true (16)
       k) true (18)
       l) true (7 is prime)
       m) true
       n) false (stays 8)

M12. For a square with this side (cm), find P (cm) and A (cm²). Then say for
     which side P and A are the same number, for which P is bigger, and for
     which A is bigger.
     a) side 1               b) side 2               c) side 3
     d) side 4               e) side 5               f) side 6
     g) side 7               h) side 8               i) side 9
     j) side 10              k) side 11              l) side 12
     m) P = A when side is   n) P > A when side is   o) A > P when side is
     Answers: a) P 4, A 1   b) P 8, A 4   c) P 12, A 9   d) P 16, A 16
              e) P 20, A 25   f) P 24, A 36   g) P 28, A 49   h) P 32, A 64
              i) P 36, A 81   j) P 40, A 100   k) P 44, A 121   l) P 48, A 144
              m) 4   n) 1, 2, 3   o) 5 and more

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: work with square tiles or matchsticks on squared paper. Build a
  shape, count the squares (area) and then walk a finger round the outside edges
  counting them (perimeter). Use two colours: one for the edge, one for the
  inside. Keep to small numbers (areas up to 12, perimeters up to 16). Harder:
  find all rectangles for areas up to 100 and spot which areas give only one
  rectangle (the primes) and which give a square (the square numbers); make a
  table of length, breadth and area for perimeter 40 and draw a bar graph of the
  areas; design a garden with the most area for 30 m of fence (7 × 8 or, with
  half metres, 7 1/2 × 7 1/2).


================================================================================
TOPIC 4.31 — AREA OF COMPOSITE (L-SHAPED) FIGURES
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - A COMPOSITE (rectilinear) figure is made of rectangles joined together, like
    an L, a T or a U. All its corners are right angles.
  - METHOD 1 — SPLIT AND ADD: cut the shape into rectangles with a straight
    line, find each area, and add. An L can be cut ACROSS or DOWN; both ways
    give the same total.
  - METHOD 2 — SUBTRACT: draw the big rectangle round the whole shape, find its
    area, and take away the area of the missing piece.
  - MISSING LENGTHS: the sides facing one way add up to the long side facing the
    same way. In an L, the bottom = top + step, and the left side = the two
    right-hand parts added.
  - Never count a piece twice: the rectangles you split into must not overlap.
  - Perimeter of an L-shape = perimeter of the rectangle round it (2 × (length +
    breadth)). Find all the missing sides first and add them.
  - A PATH or BORDER round a rectangle: area of path = area of the big rectangle
    − area of the inside rectangle. A path w m wide adds 2 × w to each side.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Here is the plan of an L-shaped room. Find its area by cutting it ACROSS
    into two rectangles.
                   4 m
              +-----------+
              |           | 3 m
              |           |     ?
              |           +-----------+
          7 m |                       | 4 m
              |                       |
              +-----------------------+
                         9 m
    Answer: Area = 12 + 36 = 48 m².
            +-------+
            |  A    |  A = 4 × 3 = 12 m²
            +-------+-----------+
            |     B             |  B = 9 × 4 = 36 m²
            +-------------------+
    Teacher explains: Draw a line across, along the bottom of the step. The top
    rectangle A is 4 m wide and 3 m tall: 4 × 3 = 12 m². The bottom rectangle B
    is the full width 9 m and 4 m tall: 9 × 4 = 36 m². Add: 12 + 36 = 48 m².
    Shade the two parts in two colours so the child can see they fit together
    with no gap and no overlap. Now you try: an L with top 5 m, step 3 m down,
    bottom 8 m, right side 4 m, left side 7 m (5 × 3 + 8 × 4 = 15 + 32 = 47 m²).

D2. Sunil cuts the same room (from D1) DOWN instead of across. Does he get the
    same area?
    Answer: Yes. Left part: 4 × 7 = 28 m². Right part: 5 × 4 = 20 m². Total 28 +
    20 = 48 m² — the same.
    Teacher explains: Cut down along the step. The left rectangle is 4 m wide
    and the full height 7 m: 4 × 7 = 28. The right rectangle needs its width:
    the step is 9 − 4 = 5 m, and its height is 4 m: 5 × 4 = 20. Total 48 m². The
    area of the room does not change with the way you cut it, so doing it both
    ways is a good CHECK. Now you try: the L in D1's "Now you try", cut down (5
    × 7 + 3 × 4 = 35 + 12 = 47 m²).

D3. Find the area of this shape by SUBTRACTING.
                   6 m
              +-----------+
              |           | 2 m
              |           |    4 m
              |           +-----------+
          7 m |                       | 5 m
              |                       |
              +-----------------------+
                        10 m
    Answer: Big rectangle 10 × 7 = 70 m². Missing corner 4 × 2 = 8 m². Area = 70
    − 8 = 62 m².
    Teacher explains: Imagine the shape is a full rectangle with a corner bitten
    off. The full rectangle is 10 m by 7 m = 70 m². The bitten corner is 4 m
    wide (the step) and 2 m tall (the drop): 8 m². Take it away: 70 − 8 = 62 m².
    Check by splitting across: 6 × 2 = 12 and 10 × 5 = 50, and 12 + 50 = 62.
    Same! Now you try: a 12 m by 8 m rectangle with a 5 m by 3 m corner missing
    (96 − 15 = 81 m²).

D4. Two sides of this L-shaped plot are not marked. Find them, then find the
    area.
                   6 m
              +-----------+
              |           | ?
              |           |     ?
              |           +-----------+
         10 m |                       | 4 m
              |                       |
              +-----------------------+
                        15 m
    Answer: Step = 15 − 6 = 9 m. Drop = 10 − 4 = 6 m. Area = 6 × 10 + 9 × 4 = 60
    + 36 = 96 m².
    Teacher explains: Look at the sides going across: the bottom (15 m) is as
    long as the top (6 m) and the step together, so the step is 15 − 6 = 9 m.
    Now the sides going up and down: the left side (10 m) is as long as the two
    right-hand pieces together, so the drop is 10 − 4 = 6 m. Now cut down: 6 ×
    10 = 60 and 9 × 4 = 36, total 96 m². Check by subtracting: 15 × 10 = 150,
    150 − 9 × 6 = 150 − 54 = 96. Now you try: bottom 12 m, top 5 m, left 9 m,
    right 3 m (step 7 m, drop 6 m; area 5 × 9 + 7 × 3 = 45 + 21 = 66 m²).

D5. What is the perimeter of the L-shaped room in D1? Why is it the same as the
    perimeter of the 9 m by 7 m rectangle round it?
    Answer: Perimeter = 4 + 3 + 5 + 4 + 9 + 7 = 32 m, and 2 × (9 + 7) = 32 m
    too.
    Teacher explains: First find every side: top 4, drop 3, step 5, right 4,
    bottom 9, left 7. Add them: 32 m. Now push the step up and out to the
    corner: the step (5 m) moves up to join the top, making 9 m, and the drop (3
    m) moves right to join the right side, making 7 m. The edge is just as long,
    but now it is the rectangle 9 by 7. So for an L, perimeter = 2 × (bottom +
    left). But the AREA does change: 48 is less than 63. Now you try: the L in
    D3 (2 × (10 + 7) = 34 m).

D6. A school garden is shaped like a T. Find its area.
                        12 m
              +-----------------------+
              |                       | 3 m
              +-------+       +-------+
                      |       |
                      |       | 6 m
                      |       |
                      +-------+
                         4 m
    Answer: Top bar 12 × 3 = 36 m². Stem 4 × 6 = 24 m². Area = 36 + 24 = 60 m².
    Teacher explains: Split the T where the stem meets the bar. The bar is a
    rectangle 12 m by 3 m: 36 m². The stem is 4 m wide and 6 m tall: 24 m². Add:
    60 m². A common mistake is to use 3 + 6 = 9 m as the height of the whole T
    and multiply 12 × 9 = 108 — that counts the two empty corners beside the
    stem. Now you try: a T with bar 10 m by 2 m and stem 3 m wide, 5 m tall (20
    + 15 = 35 m²).

D7. A school building is shaped like a U round a courtyard. The whole block is
    12 m by 8 m; the gap (the courtyard) is 4 m wide and 5 m deep. Find the area
    of the building.
                4 m      4 m      4 m
              +-----+         +-----+
              |     |    5 m  |     |
              |     |         |     |
              |     +---------+     |
          8 m |                     |
              |                     |
              +---------------------+
                       12 m
    Answer: Area = 12 × 8 − 4 × 5 = 96 − 20 = 76 m².
    Teacher explains: Subtracting is quickest here. The big rectangle is 12 × 8
    = 96 m². The gap is a rectangle 4 m by 5 m = 20 m². 96 − 20 = 76 m². Check
    by splitting: two arms 4 × 8 = 32 each, and the middle piece under the gap
    is 4 m wide and 8 − 5 = 3 m tall: 12. 32 + 32 + 12 = 76. Same! Now you try:
    a U 10 m by 6 m with a gap 2 m wide and 4 m deep (60 − 8 = 52 m²).

D8. A lawn 10 m by 6 m has a path 1 m wide all round the outside. What is the
    area of the path?
        +---------------------------+
        |       path 1 m wide       |
        |   +-------------------+   |
        |   |        lawn       |   |
        |   |     10 m × 6 m    |   |
        |   +-------------------+   |
        |                           |
        +---------------------------+
    Answer: Outside rectangle 12 m by 8 m = 96 m². Lawn = 60 m². Path = 96 − 60
    = 36 m².
    Teacher explains: The path adds 1 m on EACH side, so the length grows by 1 +
    1 = 2: 10 + 2 = 12 m, and the breadth grows by 2: 6 + 2 = 8 m. The most
    common mistake is to add only 1 m. Lawn and path together: 12 × 8 = 96. Take
    away the lawn: 96 − 60 = 36 m². Check: two long strips 12 × 1 = 12 each and
    two short strips 6 × 1 = 6 each: 12 + 12 + 6 + 6 = 36. Now you try: a pond 8
    m by 5 m with a 1 m path round it (10 × 7 − 40 = 30 m²).

D9. A photo 20 cm by 15 cm is put in a frame that is 2 cm wide all round. What
    is the area of the frame?
    Answer: Outside: 24 cm by 19 cm = 456 cm². Photo: 20 × 15 = 300 cm². Frame =
    456 − 300 = 156 cm².
    Teacher explains: Add 2 cm on both sides: 20 + 4 = 24 and 15 + 4 = 19. Work
    out 24 × 19 with the area model: 24 × 20 = 480, minus 24 = 456. The photo is
    300 cm². Frame = 456 − 300 = 156 cm². Draw it to see the frame as a border
    and the photo as the hole in the middle. Now you try: a 10 cm by 8 cm card
    with a 1 cm frame (12 × 10 − 80 = 40 cm²).

D10. Carpet for this L-shaped hall costs ₹200 for each m². How much will it cost
     to carpet the hall?
                    5 m
               +-----------+
               |           | 2 m
               |           |    3 m
               |           +-----------+
           6 m |                       | 4 m
               |                       |
               +-----------------------+
                          8 m
     Answer: Area = 5 × 2 + 8 × 4 = 10 + 32 = 42 m². Cost = 42 × ₹200 = ₹8,400.
     Teacher explains: Two steps: first the area, then the cost. Cut across: top
     part 5 × 2 = 10, bottom part 8 × 4 = 32, total 42 m². Check by subtracting:
     8 × 6 = 48, missing corner 3 × 2 = 6, and 48 − 6 = 42. Then cost: 42 × 200
     = 8,400, so ₹8,400. Now you try: an L of 30 m² at ₹150 per m² (₹4,500).

D11. For the room in D1, Neha found 4 × 7 = 28 and 9 × 4 = 36, and added them to
     get 64 m². What went wrong?
     Answer: Her two rectangles overlap: the bottom-left 4 m by 4 m square (16
     m²) was counted twice. 64 − 16 = 48 m², the correct area.
     Teacher explains: Draw her two rectangles on the plan in two colours. The
     tall left one (4 × 7) and the wide bottom one (9 × 4) share a corner piece
     4 m by 4 m. The pieces we add must fit together like a jigsaw, with no
     piece used twice. Fix it by making the second rectangle only the part NOT
     yet covered: 5 × 4 = 20, and 28 + 20 = 48. Now you try: an L with top 3,
     drop 2, step 4, right 3 (bottom 7, left 5). What is wrong with 3 × 5 + 7 ×
     3? (the 3 × 3 corner is counted twice; 15 + 12 = 27 m²)

D12. On squared paper, Aman draws a staircase shape. Each # is 1 cm². Find the
     area using rectangles, then check by counting.
         # # # . . . . . .
         # # # . . . . . .
         # # # # # # . . .
         # # # # # # . . .
         # # # # # # # # #
         # # # # # # # # #
     Answer: Rows in pairs: 3 × 2 + 6 × 2 + 9 × 2 = 6 + 12 + 18 = 36 cm².
     Teacher explains: Cut the staircase into three flat rectangles, one for
     each step. Top step: 3 wide, 2 tall: 6. Middle step: 6 wide, 2 tall: 12.
     Bottom step: 9 wide, 2 tall: 18. Total 36 cm². Counting row by row: 3 + 3 +
     6 + 6 + 9 + 9 = 36. Or subtract: the full 9 by 6 rectangle is 54 cm², the
     empty part above the steps is 6 × 2 + 3 × 2 = 18 cm², and 54 − 18 = 36 cm².
     Now you try: steps 2, 4, 6 wide, each 1 tall (2 + 4 + 6 = 12 cm²).

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Each # is 1 cm². Split each shape into rectangles and find its area. Check
    by counting. Then find the perimeter.
    a)         b)           c)         d)
    # # . .    # # # # #    # . . #    # # # . .
    # # . .    . . # . .    # . . #    # # # . .
    # # # #    . . # . .    # # # #    # # # # #
                                       # # # # #

    e)             f)         g)           h)
    # # # # # #    . # # .    # # # # #    . . # # # #
    # # # # # #    # # # #    # # # # #    . . # # # #

             d) A 16 cm², P 18 cm   e) A 20 cm², P 24 cm   f) A 12 cm², P 16 cm

M2. All the L-shapes in M2 to M5 and M12 are drawn like this (lengths in m,
    areas in m²):
                p
          +-----------+

    Find t, u, the area A and the perimeter P.
    a) p 4, q 3, r 5, s 4     b) p 6, q 2, r 4, s 5     c) p 3, q 5, r 7, s 2
    d) p 8, q 4, r 2, s 6     e) p 5, q 5, r 5, s 5     f) p 2, q 6, r 8, s 3

    a) t 9, u 7, p 4, s 4       b) t 15, u 10, p 6, s 4

             g) q 6, r 6, A 85   h) q 3, r 6, A 62   i) q 4, r 9, A 76
             j) q 4, r 9, A 29   k) q 8, r 10, A 420   l) q 1, r 5, A 49

M4. The whole rectangle is t by u and the missing corner is r by q. Find the
    area A = t × u − r × q.
    a) t 9, u 7, q 3, r 5       b) t 10, u 8, q 2, r 6

             j) 35 − 1 = 34   k) 198 − 36 = 162   l) 300 − 35 = 265

M5. Find the area both ways: ACROSS (p × q + t × s) and DOWN (p × u + r × s).
    The answers must agree.
    a) p 4, q 3, r 5, s 4    b) p 6, q 2, r 4, s 5    c) p 3, q 5, r 7, s 2
    d) p 8, q 4, r 2, s 6    e) p 5, q 1, r 4, s 6    f) p 2, q 6, r 8, s 3
    g) p 10, q 3, r 5, s 7   h) p 7, q 2, r 3, s 4    i) p 9, q 4, r 1, s 5
    j) p 6, q 6, r 6, s 6
    Answers:
      a) across 4 × 3 + 9 × 4 = 12 + 36 = 48; down 4 × 7 + 5 × 4 = 28 + 20 = 48
      b) across 6 × 2 + 10 × 5 = 12 + 50 = 62; down 6 × 7 + 4 × 5 = 42 + 20 = 62
      c) across 3 × 5 + 10 × 2 = 15 + 20 = 35; down 3 × 7 + 7 × 2 = 21 + 14 = 35
      d) across 8 × 4 + 10 × 6 = 32 + 60 = 92; down 8 × 10 + 2 × 6 = 80 + 12 =
         92
      e) across 5 × 1 + 9 × 6 = 5 + 54 = 59; down 5 × 7 + 4 × 6 = 35 + 24 = 59
      f) across 2 × 6 + 10 × 3 = 12 + 30 = 42; down 2 × 9 + 8 × 3 = 18 + 24 = 42
      g) across 10 × 3 + 15 × 7 = 30 + 105 = 135; down 10 × 10 + 5 × 7 = 100 +
         35 = 135
      h) across 7 × 2 + 10 × 4 = 14 + 40 = 54; down 7 × 6 + 3 × 4 = 42 + 12 = 54
      i) across 9 × 4 + 10 × 5 = 36 + 50 = 86; down 9 × 9 + 1 × 5 = 81 + 5 = 86
      j) across 6 × 6 + 12 × 6 = 36 + 72 = 108; down 6 × 12 + 6 × 6 = 72 + 36 =
         108

M6. T-shapes, drawn like this (bar A by B, stem C wide and D tall):
                      A
          +-----------------------+
          |                       | B
          +-------+       +-------+
                  |       |

    a) A 12, B 3, C 4, D 6     b) A 10, B 2, C 2, D 5

M7. U-shapes: the whole block is W by H; the gap in the top is g wide and d

M8. A rectangle with a rectangular hole (like a garden with a pond). Outer

M12. Work backwards. The area A of each L-shape is p × u + r × s. Find the
     missing length.

     c) p 5, u ?, r 4, s 3, A 47       d) p 3, u ?, r 6, s 2, A 30
